**79. Revision 79 (2026-10-04).** §4.3 gains seam 9, `Observer`, php's observer interface, and revision 58's sentence on what the executor-hooked switch serves is replaced, settled by Mitchell on #1776 for #1770. Revision 58's sentence counted observers and profilers among the tiers that switch serves, which is wrong for an observer, since an observer changes no call opcode: php's `zend_get_call_op` reads `zend_execute_ex` and `zend_execute_internal` alone (`Z/Zend/zend_compile.c:3919-3949`). The sentence now reads "the same switch serves any later tier that replaces the executor, as phpdbg and xdebug's step debugger do in php; an observer changes no call opcode (`zend_get_call_op`, `Z/Zend/zend_compile.c:3919-3949`, reads `zend_execute_ex` and `zend_execute_internal` alone) and is seam 9's", in revision 58's entry and in §4.3's paragraph on seam 8. Row 9 and the paragraph after seam 8's carry the ruling's owner, declaration, per-function slot, ordering, run-time add and remove, check, pairing, notices, frameless fallback, handler argument, escape and php counterpart, in its words. `jit-design.md` revision 11 says what compiled code emits at observed calls. The ruling names no adapters, so row 9's Adapters cell waits on #1906. Rejected: an ADR, for a seam that reverses nothing; a checked `FrameRef` handed to handlers now, which fixes #1827's design before #697's ruling on views is applied and costs a check per read; and striking revision 58's clause without the corrected reason. ADR-0006 stands; nothing is reversed, so there is no ADR. Check: a unit compiled by an engine with an observer declared carries the call opcodes it carries with none, as php's `zend_get_call_op` picks them; and `grep -n 'observer or profiler tier' third-engine-design.md` finds nothing.

**80. Revision 80 (2026-10-05).** §4.1 gains the `php_process` row and its `unsafe` home, settled by Mitchell on #1779 for #1770: a `php_process` crate is the one home for `fork`. `php_engine` forbids `unsafe` and `fork` is `unsafe`, so the syscall cannot live in the engine; its policy can. Revision 38 kept the signal pool in `php_heap` and rejected a `php_process` crate, which signals and `LC_CTYPE` were the only instances of, saying the pool could move into one unchanged if more arrived; fork is what arrived, so revision 38's condition is met. `php_process` is a leaf crate below `php_engine`, an `unsafe` home of its own, holding the process-wide mechanisms: `fork`, the signal pool (`php_heap::post`, moved unchanged) and `LC_CTYPE` (`php_heap::reset_lc_ctype_locale`). `php_engine` holds the policy: it joins every thread it owns, restores in the child what php's `php_child_init` restores, and re-arms its timer in the parent; pcntl asks through the request, hosts through `php_sapi` (#1807). `unsafe` is permitted in seven places, and `heap.lock` loses `post::*` and `reset_lc_ctype_locale` to `process.lock`, the `process` seam's lock, whose one consumer is `php_engine`; `seams.toml` carries the crate's row, the seam and the `fork.home` rule, which denies a bare `fork` outside `crates/php_process`. ADR-0006's sentence on the homes is amended to name the seven. Rejected: `fork` in `php_heap` beside the pool, a second process primitive in the memory model's crate; `unsafe fork` left in pcntl's and fpm's `ffi` behind a token, two homes no pattern rule holds. Nothing that an ADR records is reversed, so there is no ADR. Check: `grep -n 'post::\|reset_lc_ctype_locale' crates/php_heap/heap.lock` finds nothing, `cargo xtask unsafe-audit` passes `php_process` as a home, and the `fork.home` fixture under `xtask/fixtures/rules/` is denied.

**81. Revision 81 (2026-10-05).** §4.1 gains the `php_streams` row and the `php_engine` row drops streams, and §4.3 names `php_streams` as seam 5's owner, settled by Mitchell on #1874 after #1773 ruled the split: php's `main/streams/` and `main/network.c`, the wrappers, transports, filters, the user wrapper and the stream context, sit above `php_engine` over `api`, as php's `Zend/` reaches streams only through `zend_stream_open_function`. The engine reaches the layer through the open hook `ext/standard` hands it (`StreamHooks`, a supplied kind beside `zend_resolve_path`'s chain) and through request state; `api.lock` stays the one face `php_ext` prints, `php_streams` a second producer whose items `php_ext` re-exports into its request group (#1875). Rejected: `php_streams` below the engine, which would need the request as a trait (ADR-0002), and a `streams.lock` of its own. Nothing is reversed, so there is no ADR. Check: `php_engine` has no `streams` or `wrapper` module, and `cargo xtask api-check` passes with `php_streams`' items counted in `api.lock`.

**82. Revision 82 (2026-10-05).** §4.10's mysqlnd row names `client::Idle`, settled by Mitchell on #1674 and landed for #1727 with #1805: "`client::Idle`, an idle persistent connection, what `mysqlnd.h`'s persistent `MYSQLND *` is between requests: it owns the connection's `ConnData`, and a pool entry of pdo_mysql or mysqli holds it, so dropping the entry closes the connection." One persistent mysqli or pdo_mysql connection had waited in three pools between requests, its owner's entry holding a key, mysqlnd's own engine-state pool the `ConnData` and the engine's persistent streams the socket, where php's `mysqli_plist_entry` or `pdo_dbh_t` holds the `MYSQLND` itself. mysqlnd's pool is removed and `client::Mysqlnd::give_back` answers an `Idle`. The persistent stream is not observable through php's persistent list, since `mysqlnd_vio::open_tcp_or_unix` deletes it from `EG(persistent_list)` and `EG(regular_list)` (`Z/ext/mysqlnd/mysqlnd_vio.c:139-169`), so the socket moves into `Idle` too, through the streams facet's `Stream::keep`, and one drop frees everything. The ruling names no rejected option. Nothing is reversed, so there is no ADR. Check: `cargo xtask api-check` prints `client::Idle` among `ext/mysqlnd`'s items beyond `extension()` and nothing this row does not name, and `grep -n 'struct Idle(Pool' ext/mysqlnd/src/lib.rs` finds nothing.

**83. Revision 83 (2026-10-05).** §7.1 gains four refusals beside "There is no `dl()`", settled by Mitchell on #1780 for #1770 and typed in the ruling's words: on opcode handlers, syntax-tree processing, the allocator's handlers and the Zend-extension message handler, each saying what stands in its place. The first three cite principles by `AGENTS.md`'s numbering, under which §3's principles 2, 1 and 4 are 1, 2 and 3, and the second cites ADR-0003, which says nothing of publication; #1963 asks which citations §7.1 carries. Opcode observation and allocation observation are built because php's own tests exercise them through its test extension (#1782): `ext/opcache/tests/jit/ignored_opcodes.phpt` observes opcodes, the one php test that sets `zend_test.observer.show_opcode_in_user_handler`, and `zend_mm_custom_handlers_{alloc,free,gc}_01.phpt` drive the allocator hooks; outside them their only users are tools that cannot be loaded here (revision 107, #2032). The same ruling settles `-e` (#1822) and a regime's arguments in `oracle.toml` (#1933), neither of which is a §7.1 sentence. Rejected: refusals that name no equivalent; building either observation for tools with no user; refusing them while php's own tests exercise them. Nothing is reversed, so there is no ADR. Check: `api.lock` names no item through which an extension replaces an opcode's handler, rewrites a published unit's tree or replaces `php_heap::mm`; `ignored_opcodes.phpt`'s program, served without its JIT ini under the `zend-test` variant, writes the oracle's bytes, and `crates/php_engine/src/tests/opcodes.rs` holds the sequences the oracle prints, the handler still the engine's (revision 107, #2032); and once #1782 ports it, `zend_mm_custom_handlers_alloc_01.phpt` meets its expectation by observation, the allocator still the engine's.

**84. Revision 84 (2026-10-05).** §4.10's list of surfaces drops the stream context's items, settled by Mitchell on #1777 for #1770: `php_streams` owns the stream context and the default context, beside `Stream`, as php keeps `php_stream_context` in `main/streams/`. The rows `standard` → `zlib`, `zip`, `libxml`, `phar`, `soap` and `openssl` drop `default_stream_ctx`, `stream_ctx_option`, `stream_ctx_set_option` and `stream_ctx_alloc`, which those extensions reach through `php_ext` from `php_streams`; each row keeps its other items, and a row left with none is removed (none is). Where php stores the default context (`FG(default_context)`) is not observable, so it is held in `php_streams`' request state; ext/standard's `stream_context_*` natives reach both through `php_ext`, and transports read their options (`socket.bindto`) directly (#1818). Rejected: ext/standard keeping the payload behind a registered reader, which leaves two owners and puts a hook between a transport and an option beside it; the default context left in ext/standard behind a hook, which copies php's storage where storage is not observable. Nothing is reversed, so there is no ADR. Check: `grep -n 'stream_ctx_\|default_stream_ctx' third-engine-design.md ext/*/src` finds only this entry, and a `SoapClient` under `socket.bindto` connects from the bound port, as the oracle's does.

**85. Revision 85 (2026-10-05).** The bundle's order is php's, settled by Mitchell on #1778 for #1770 and landed by #1821: copied, with php's sort. The engine's module order differed from the pinned build's for 55 of 58 extensions, visible in `get_loaded_extensions()`, `get_defined_functions()`, `get_declared_classes()` and `get_defined_constants(true)`, and a 65-name table in the stream layer patched the order of wrappers and filters by hand. `php_bundle`'s `enabled()` now lists the extensions in php's registration order for the oracle's enabled set (configure's `order_by_dep.awk` over `config0`/`config`/`config9` priority, directory order and `PHP_ADD_EXTENSION_DEP`), and `EngineBuilder::build` sorts them as `zend_sort_modules` does over each extension's declared `dependencies` (php's `ZEND_MOD_REQUIRED`/`OPTIONAL`/`CONFLICTS`); it fills the function table in registration order before the sort, as `zend_register_module_ex` does, and starts, activates and lists the extensions in sorted order, so the stream layer appends what each activation registers and its table and rank go. An extension's engine start reads another extension's state only if it declares that dependency. Cargo edges are compile-time and no longer constrain the order. §4.10 **Order**, §4.1's `php_bundle` cell and §4.2's `api-check` sentence carry the ruling's sentences word for word; `php_bundle`'s and `api-check`'s rustdoc say the same; the manifest's `[[edge]] php_bundle` row reads `order = "registration"` (0a3809e5, a person's hand). ADR-0004 states no order and is not amended. Rejected: the oracle's literal list captured as data with no sort, which places an extension the oracle lacks by hand; keeping topological order with a divergence row for four observable listings. Nothing that an ADR records is reversed, so there is no ADR. Check: `php_bundle`'s witness on `enabled()` holds the four listings to the oracle for the bundled set, so an oracle bump that moves the order fails there; `grep -n 'MODULE_STARTUP\|startup_rank' crates/` finds nothing.

**86. Revision 86 (2026-10-05).** §7.1's four refusals cite principles by §3's number and name, settled by Mitchell on #1963 for #1770. Revision 83 typed #1780's ruled sentences, whose citations followed `AGENTS.md`'s four-item list (its 1, 2 and 3 are §3's 2, 1 and 4), so three pointed at the wrong principle of §3, the list the design cites everywhere else ("principle 9", "principle 12"). The opcode-handler refusal now cites §3's principle 2, the allocator refusal principle 1, and the syntax-tree refusal principle 4, on its real ground: a unit is a function of its source and its compile identity, shared by every engine in the process, so a tree rewritten per extension would put the extension set in every unit's identity, the cost #1954 found for observer-specialised handlers. ADR-0003 is dropped from that sentence, since it is about the tree's representation and says nothing of publication. A principle is cited as "(§3, principle N: name)", so a stale number shows beside its name, and `AGENTS.md`'s list carries §3's numbers so that a number copied from it is §3's. Rejected: two numberings in one document; renumbering §3, which breaks every existing citation; keeping ADR-0003 as "there is one tree". Check: `grep -n "(principle [0-9]" third-engine-design.md` finds no §7.1 refusal, and each of the three cites its §3 name.

**87. Revision 87 (2026-10-06).** §5.1 gains the ownership instruments' paragraph, settled by Mitchell on #1980 (2026-10-05) for candidate 1 and landed by #1993, typed in the ruling's words: "The freed-use guard stands at the refcounted header's increment and decrement, the one chokepoint every refcount operation reaches, and a refused operation is reported at the next sensing point and at request end, never dropped. A debug build quarantines each bin's last sixteen freed slots, first in first out, checking each slot's mark and first word as it leaves, and drains the quarantine before the limit is consulted. The request leak gate is php's debug shutdown: the shutdown GC before object storage is freed, request state dropped before the heap, every request-lifetime block freed by name, and then an empty heap, or the request ends in an `InternalError` naming the extension that allocated what is left; it does not run after a fatal." The paragraph realises the sentence of "Ownership, decided" that the cost of explicit refcounts "is paid by the instrument, not by callers", which named an instrument the code did not have. Each clause has its ticket: the guard at the header's increment and decrement, reached by every type-level addref, is #1990's; the quarantine is #1991's; php's debug shutdown and the empty heap are #1992's, armed by `EngineBuilder::sense_leaks` and, in debug hosts, by `PHP_SENSE_LEAKS=1`, and off by default until the leaks #2057 counts are fixed; the extension a leak names is #1993's, the `module_number` of the extension whose native or lifecycle hook was being served when the block was handed out, kept per block beside the chunk rather than in the block, so `memory_get_usage()` stays the oracle's release build's (`php_heap::Origin`). #1993 also adds `PHP_SENSE_LEAKS_BACKTRACE=1`, which keeps a backtrace per allocation and names the frames that allocated the first block left. The code calls the gate the request leak sensor, since `CONTEXT.md` keeps "gate" for a numbered bar. Nothing is reversed, so there is no ADR. Check: an addref through any type-level path on a freed block is refused and reported; using the most recently freed slot of its bin is reported on the first run; a native that drops an owner fails its test naming its extension (`crates/php_engine/src/tests/leak_sensor.rs`, "allocated by leak_sensor"); the phpt pass count under the gate equals the count without it. Measured at #1993's branch, the last does not hold yet: `Zend/tests` passes 5200 of 5308 unarmed, and armed 46 tests miss their expectation and 111 skip where 107 did, where #1992's census for #2057 counted 47 misses, so it waits on #2057's causes and on #1992's default flip. #1992 flipped it once those causes were fixed: the sensor is armed by default in every build with debug assertions, `EngineBuilder::sense_leaks` turns it off, and so does `PHP_SENSE_LEAKS=0` in a debug host. Measured on the flip, the last holds: every subset of the per-commit lane meets and misses the same tests armed as unarmed, `Zend/tests` 5200 of 5308 and `tests` 866 of 867 either way, and `xtask witness` passes the same 75 crates and 6264 witnesses armed as unarmed.

**88. Revision 88 (2026-10-06).** §5.3 says how a table is copied, settled by Mitchell on #1980 (2026-10-05) for #2005 and typed in the ruling's words: "Copying a table as php copies it is one rule beside `array_dup`: `zend_array_dup_element`'s unwrapping of a reference with one owner, and `zend_symtable_to_proptable`'s sharing of a table whose keys are all strings. `$GLOBALS`, `get_defined_vars`, the `(object)` cast and the property-table copy use it, and `memory_get_usage()` is held to php's across them." php's rule for an element of a copy is restated in four builders here, and two had drifted: `get_defined_vars()` kept a reference only the variable held, so writing to its result after `sort()` or `extract(EXTR_REFS)` wrote the variable, and the `(object)` cast copied a string-keyed array element by element where php takes the array as the object's table, 41,056 bytes against php's 40 for a thousand elements. `php_heap::table_copy` owns the rule beside `Heap::array_dup`: `Heap::dup_value` is `zend_array_dup_value`, and `Heap::symtable_to_proptable` and `Heap::proptable_to_symtable` are php's two conversions, each handing the table itself on where its keys allow it and building a copy under the rule otherwise. `$GLOBALS` and `get_defined_vars()` copy the engine's symbol tables row by row through it, the `(object)` cast lays down what `symtable_to_proptable` answers, and the `(array)` cast, `get_object_vars()` and `get_mangled_object_vars()` share an object's table where php does, through `proptable_to_symtable`, and copy rows through the rule where it does not. What `$GLOBALS` and `get_defined_vars()` cost is not php's yet: a symbol table here keeps its names as bytes, so a copy makes a string per name where php's adds an owner to the table's own, and it is sized by its count where `zend_array_dup` takes the source's size, which #2108 builds; `ext/mysqli`'s `fetch_object` keeps a conversion of its own, which #2110 moves onto the rule. The ruling names no rejected option. Nothing is reversed, so there is no ADR. Check: writing to `get_defined_vars()`'s result changes no variable; a kept `(object)` cast of a string-keyed array costs what php's costs.

**89. Revision 89 (2026-10-06).** §5.6 cites the tracing JIT's entry counter, `zend_jit_setup_hot_trace_counters` (`Z/ext/opcache/jit/zend_jit_trace.c:8983-9001`), where it cited the function JIT's (`zend_jit.c:3219-3231`); both place the counter by the same rule, and the tracing JIT's is the one the compiled tier copies. The risk table names the entry-counter difference a `trace` row of the registry, not the third: the two rows beside it, for a tier not yet built, left the registry for `jit-design.md` §4.1 (its revision 12), settled by Mitchell on #2142. Nothing is reversed, so there is no ADR. Check: `docs/divergence.toml` holds `trace:entry-counter-typed-recv` and cites the same lines.

**90. Revision 90 (2026-10-06).** §5.14 gains the *Identities* paragraph and §6.2's run-time link goes through an inheritance cache, settled by Mitchell on #2176 (2026-10-06) and typed as settled for #2180. A class or a method published with a unit, or by the inheritance cache, is named by a row of an engine-wide, append-only published arena, and `ClassId` and `Function` stay one `u32` whose top bit says published or the request's own. `DECLARE_CLASS_DELAYED` first looks for a published linked class keyed on the unlinked declaration and the published identities of its dependencies, as `zend_accel_inheritance_cache_find` does, and publishes what it links where every dependency is published; §6.2 had said it "links at run time into request-owned classes". Why: the measured case is all run-time links, and a run-time link per request leaves every composer-autoloaded class at `php -n`'s cost at best; an inheritance cache needs its dependencies to have stable identities, which per-request numbers are not. A replaced unit's rows are counted as wasted and cleared by an opcache restart once no request holds the arena's epoch. #2181 (identities), #2182 (early-bound classes published), #2183 (the inheritance cache) and #2184 (the restart) build it. Rejected: map-pointer identities, since a map pointer is for per-request mutable state and a name's target is not state; `Arc<LinkedClass>` identities, for their 8-byte handles and refcount traffic; linking per request only, and publishing early-bound classes alone, which never publishes a run-time link; generational slot reuse and per-class refcounts for reclaim. Nothing an ADR records is reversed, so there is no ADR. Check: a request that declares a cached early-bound class allocates no method row, and a second request's run-time link of the same class performs no link.

**91. Revision 91 (2026-10-06).** §10's **Profiles** bullet names the three profiles `Cargo.toml` has, settled by Mitchell on #2055 (2026-10-06), "as built", and typed in the ruling's words. The bullet named two: dev at `opt-level = 1`, where `[profile.dev]` sets no `opt-level` and cargo's 0 stands, with line-table debug info, packed split debug info and no incremental cache for disk (#839, #1609); and release, as built. It did not name `release-da`, the build every correctness lane runs since harness-design.md revision 82 (#1994). Dev now reads `opt-level = 0` with what it sets for disk, and `release-da` is named with harness-design.md §7, which states its rule. `Cargo.toml` does not change. Rejected, by the ruling: dev at `opt-level = 1` as written, which would have changed `Cargo.toml`. Nothing is reversed, so there is no ADR. Check: `Cargo.toml`'s `[profile.dev]`, `[profile.release]` and `[profile.release-da]` read as the bullet says, and no fourth profile stands beside them.

**92. Revision 92 (2026-10-07).** §4.4 gains *Walking a table*, settled by Mitchell on #1980 (2026-10-05) for candidate 3, revised on #2106 and #2114 and again on #2159 (2026-10-06), and typed in the ruling's words for #2004: "A native walks a table in one of four ways. A rows walk borrows the request, so a body that can run user code does not compile in it. A foreach walk reads the table's rows as they stand, up to where they ended as it began, as php's `ZEND_HASH_FOREACH` does; it holds the table and guards its recursion where php's native does, and where it holds nothing it ends at the table's destruction rather than read it freed. A Live walk follows php's hash iterator, re-reading the holder after each step and raising php's `TypeError` when it no longer holds an array or object. A Detached walk works on a private copy it swaps back. The three callback walks keep each step's key and value alive for the step and lend them to the callback, owning them only where php's native does and otherwise pinning them, which php's counts do not show; they take an array or an object's property table, and clean up on every exit. The engine's hold is the owner type." #2106 ruled the hold: a callback walk holds the table where php's native holds it and nowhere else, each call site naming php's choice, and where it holds nothing a hash iterator registered at the table ends the walk at the table's destruction, a recorded divergence from php's undefined behaviour rather than a diagnostic. #2114 ruled the count: four ways, the Held walk and the bucket walk merged into the foreach walk, php's `ZEND_HASH_FOREACH`. #2159 ruled the row: a walk keeps a lent row alive with an owner only where php's native takes one, and everywhere else with a pin, an uncounted guard that delays a pinned block's free, and any destructor with it, until the step ends, so php's counts and copy-on-write stand; under `Hold::Owner` the rows are lent as views, since the held table keeps them, and the Live walk keeps its owners, since `php_array_walk` copies its call arguments. `snapshot()` and `snapshot_refs()`, which handed a native views of every row with nothing keeping them while its body ran code of the program, are gone: each of the 164 callers #2000 left is on the walk php's native uses. Nothing an ADR records is reversed, so there is no ADR. Check: no `snapshot()` or `snapshot_refs()` remains; a rows-walk body calling user code fails to compile (`walk::rows`' `compile_fail` fences, `E0502`); `array_walk` matches php under unset, append and holder replacement (`walk::live`'s witness); `serialize` of #2043's snippet prints what php prints. The clause "otherwise pinning them" is pending on #2197, which builds the pin, and so is the clause lending views under `Hold::Owner`: until it lands the foreach walk owns every row it lends, whatever its hold, so where php's native holds none a count the program reads during the step is one higher than php's, and a row the program rewrites in place two tables down is separated rather than rewritten, #2159's two measured cases. The walk gives that owner up as `zval_ptr_dtor_nogc` does, so it leaves no possible root of the cycle collector php's walk does not leave.

**93. Revision 93 (2026-10-07).** §5.1 gains *Owners and views*, settled by Mitchell on #1980 (2026-10-05) for candidate 2 and typed in the ruling's words for #1999: "A native gives up exactly the owners it took and did not hand on. The `api::values` types are views wherever they are used, and one owner type, neither `Copy` nor `Clone`, must-use and dereferencing to the view it owns, is what constructors return and sinks take; one conversion takes an owner from a view, and only the engine may name an owner it did not take. In a debug build the owner type's `Drop` releases nothing and reports the site that made an owner dropped unconsumed; a release build gives it no `Drop`, so no drop glue runs, as this section decides. The handler layer over raw `Zval` keeps its own discipline." Revision 1's *Ownership, decided* stands: `Zval` and `Value` stay `Copy` with no releasing `Drop`, refcounts stay explicit and the allocator still frees everything at request end; what the revision adds is who may say a count is held, in the types. The debug `Drop` is the refinement of the section's reason, not a reversal of it: it records and releases nothing, so no build runs implicit code on drop. The migration went one sink at a time and ended here: the constructors (#2047), the array sinks and `Key::from_string` (#1996), the property sinks (#1997), the stream openers (#2104), the handler slots and `Entry` (#2102), the reference, variable and constant sinks (#2178), and last the conversions, with the scoped read `coerce::with_string`, php's `zval_get_tmp_string` and `zend_tmp_string_release`, for a value's text read once (#1999). The transitional exit that handed an owner on as a bare view, `Owner::hand_on_unmigrated`, is gone; what a native keeps past a call, in its request state, an object's or a resource's payload or a stream's kind, it keeps as an owner and gives up on that state's release path. Nothing an ADR records is reversed, so there is no ADR. Check: a view handed to a sink fails to compile (`PhpArray::set`'s, `PhpObject::set_row`'s and `PhpRef::set`'s `compile_fail` fences, `E0308`; an extension naming an owner it did not take, `Owner`'s `E0624`); an owner dropped unconsumed in a debug build ends the request in an `InternalError` naming the line that made it (php_engine's `tests::owners`); no ownership helper remains in ext/: no use of `hand_on_unmigrated` in the code, and none of the take-then-release bridges #2185 named (`owned`, `owner_of`, `owned_bare`, `copied`).

**94. Revision 94 (2026-10-07).** The harness links no workspace crate, as Mitchell settled on #2213 and landed by #2264. Since #2166 (harness revision 84) `xtask` reaches the engine through `php-linked` and the hosts' own binaries, which it builds and starts, and since ae535d99 `seams.toml` gives it no `[[edge]]` row, so `api-check` refuses any workspace dependency of it. §4.2's diagram drew `xtask ──► php_bundle`, §6.1's compile-identity paragraph said the lane compiles in this process through the bundle, and §7.1's cross-check paragraph said `xtask crosscheck` links `php_bundle`; each now says the settled sentence, word for word: "xtask links no workspace crate: it reaches the engine through the binaries it builds and starts, `php-linked` and the hosts' own (harness revision 84)." Revision 10, which chose the in-process way for compile-identity, stands as a record; what it kept open, the engine's binary dumping like php, is the way the lane now takes. Nothing that an ADR records is reversed, so there is no ADR. Check: `grep -n php_bundle third-engine-design.md` finds no passage outside the ledger saying `xtask` links or reaches the engine through it, and `cargo xtask api-check` holds `xtask` at zero workspace dependencies.

The cell now reads `enabled() -> &'static [fn() -> Extension]`, the type the code has. Rejected, by the ruling: changing the code to the design's type. Nothing is reversed, so there is no ADR. The row read "`mahout_value/src/convert.rs:121-183`, the `php_gcvt` shape | Mahout | `php_engine` kernels", and the port did not land there: `zend_gcvt` (`Z/Zend/zend_strtod.c:4509`) is `php_heap::numeric::double_to_string`, beside `_is_numeric_string_ex` as `numeric_prefix` in the same file, and `heap.lock` carries both; `php_engine::api::coerce` and `serialize_precision`'s native read it from there. **100. Revision 100 (2026-10-07).** §4.4's State table, §4.10's State, §5.14's **Threads**, §7.1, seam 3's row and `CONTEXT.md` describe the lifecycle ADR-0010 made, and name the list every lifecycle step follows the startup order, settled by Mitchell on #1907 (2026-10-07) with #1800's two sentences and #1969's exception, and on #1357 (2026-10-01) for #1067. ADR-0010 (#1774, landed by #1878) made an extension's lifecycle three scopes, process, engine and request, that start as values and end as phases, and the design still described the one it replaced: engine state declared as `engine_state(fn(&StartupIni) -> E)` and built "in `Engine::new`, in bundle order; this is `minit`", no process-state row, no `post_deactivate`, the tier's compile worker as the one engine-owned thread, and "Lifecycle order is the bundle's and its reverse", which revision 85's sort left untrue: the engine starts the bundle's list as `zend_sort_modules` leaves it. The State table gains the process-state row, built at most once per process before its extension's engine start, and names the request-end phases and `engine_state`'s `Start` and `Refusal`, as `crates/php_engine/src/api/abi.rs` declares them; §7.1 names the three scopes and their phases, #1800's two sentences on a process start's order and its refusal, as built (`engine.rs`, `process_scope.rs`), and #1969's exception, that the built-in server alone serves the request-end phases after a failed start; §5.14 says the engine owns every thread it runs, one execution-timer thread serving every request's deadline and periodic interrupt, which #2252 builds, and the tier's compile worker, each joined before a fork and at engine end; "startup order" is the one name for the bundle's list as `zend_sort_modules` leaves it, in §7.1, §4.4, §4.10's State and the shutdown order of `zval_ptr_dtor` semantics (#1357's three sentences; its first, on §4.10's **Order**, revision 85 already carries). `CONTEXT.md`'s Engine state, Request state and Interrupt entries follow, and Interrupt names its three setters, the periodic interrupt of #1806 among them, as §5.8's *The interrupt* now does in the same words. #1800's note that two extensions declaring one process-state type share one start is not stated: process state is keyed by the extension's name and the type (`process_scope.rs`), so they do not. Rejected, by ADR-0010: a process scope that sees the ini, a host-called process end, and `post_deactivate` as `Drop`. Nothing is reversed beyond what ADR-0010 records, so there is no new ADR. Check: `grep -n 'StartupIni) -> E\|this is .minit\|bundle.s and its reverse' third-engine-design.md CONTEXT.md` finds only ledger rows, and the State table's rows name the declarations `abi.rs` has.

**101. Revision 101 (2026-10-07).** §5.11's enumeration of what a fiber switch swaps names `Vm` and seam 9's chain of open observed frames, settled by Mitchell on #2013 (2026-10-07), and §4.3's seam 9 row names its adapters, settled on #1906 (2026-10-07), both landed by #2256. #1811 keeps the observer's chain, php's `EG(current_observed_frame)`, as a field of `fiber::Execution`, swapped by `Request::switch_execution` with the VM stack its frames stand on, so a frame of a suspended fiber is never ended in place of the resumer's at the same height, and `Execution` holds `Vm`, the frames' engine half, beside `VmStack` (`fiber.rs`, `exec.rs`); the enumeration named neither. php keeps the chain on the `zend_fiber_context` rather than in `zend_fiber_vm_state` and swaps it at the switch's notice (`Z/Zend/zend_observer.c:409-425`), and the section's rule holds as stated: the chain is the engine's, and what an observer keeps per fiber is its own, kept through the fiber notices. Seam 9's Adapters cell read "Not yet ruled (#1906)"; it names three: ext/zend_test's observer, driven by php's observer tests; the recording test observer of #1809; and none declared, which the loop pays nothing for, held by the bench reading #2011 takes with its A/A control. Nothing is reversed, so there is no ADR. Check: `fiber::Execution` holds `vm` and `observing` beside `stack`, and a frame observed inside a suspended fiber ends on that fiber's resume and never on the resumer's return (`crates/php_engine/src/tests/observer.rs`).

**102. Revision 102 (2026-10-07).** §6.3 says an evicted unit lives as long as the epoch, settled by Mitchell on #2186 (2026-10-07) and landed by #2225, in the ruling's words: "An evicted unit is wasted memory, as opcache's is: the epoch keeps it, with the rows it published, until the opcache restart that clears the epoch once no request holds it (§5.14 *Identities*). So nothing a request, a trace or a compile job of that epoch can reach is freed before the epoch is (`jit-design.md` §4.5)." The sentence it replaces had a published trace hold an `Arc<Published>` for every unit it inlined, so that an evicted unit died with the last trace or request holding it; revision 90's published arena already keeps any unit that published rows alive until the restart, since its `UserFunction` rows hold the unit, so there were two lifetime rules for one unit, and #2184's reported wasted figure now equals the memory held. `jit-design.md` revision 14 lands the tier's half: the trace table belongs to the epoch, and no trace is freed alone. Rejected, by the ruling: split lifetimes, which keep two rules and have every holder that embeds addresses hold units one by one; each trace holding the epoch, which keeps the sweep and needs #2215 first; per-class holds; and listing the causes that make a class request-scoped, which would go stale. Nothing an ADR records is reversed, so there is no ADR. Check: no sentence of this document or of `jit-design.md` has a trace freed alone, or a published class held by anything but the epoch.

**103. Revision 103 (2026-10-07).** §4.10's list of surfaces gains the rows #1329's XML extensions make, settled by Mitchell on #1347 (2026-10-01) with #1344, as prose rows. `libxml` → `xml`, `xmlreader`, `xmlwriter`: libxml2's SAX2 push parser as `compat.c` drives it, for ext/xml; `xmlreader.h`'s text reader with its RelaxNG and schema validation, for ext/xmlreader; `xmlwriter.h` over a memory buffer, a borrowed stream, or a file through php's wrappers, for ext/xmlwriter; the row names no items, which wait for the item hold. `standard` → `xml`, as proposed: `php_next_utf8_char`, mirroring `html.h`'s, which `xml.c` includes to decode a target encoding's characters. **The `libxml` surface** paragraph, which exported "to the other XML extensions as they land", names them. The ruling's `dom` → `xmlreader` row already stands, merged with `xsl`'s (#1564). `xtask/src/api_check/surfaces.rs` drops the entry that held `php_next_utf8_char` waiting on #1347. Nothing is reversed, so there is no ADR. Check: `cargo xtask api-check` passes ext/standard with nothing waiting on #1347, and `grep -rn 'standard::' ext/xml/src` names only `php_next_utf8_char`.

**104. Revision 104 (2026-10-07).** The linker and the linked class record move out of `php_unit` into a crate of their own, `php_link`, as Mitchell ruled on #2193 and landed by #2227. ADR-0012 records the ruling; it supersedes revision 33's placement and restores ADR-0001's format-only surface for `php_unit`. Rejected, by the ruling: raising `php_unit`'s ceiling, since one ceiling would keep measuring two things, and holding it, since every table of the record would be paid for in line-golf. Check: `php_unit` holds no `link` module and no `link::` line stands in `unit.lock`; `cargo xtask api-check` holds `link.lock` to `php_link`'s public items and finds no workspace dependency of `php_link` beyond `php_unit` and `php_heap`.

Revision 106 (2026-10-08).** The crate list, the dependency graph and every count of crates and `unsafe` homes are rendered from `seams.toml` or cited, never typed, as Mitchell ruled on #1921, #1909 and #1935 (2026-10-07), landed by #2250. **107. Revision 107 (2026-10-08).** Revision 83's sentence and check on opcode observation name the tests that observe opcodes, and §7.1 and §5.8 say the loop of an engine where no opcode is declared carries no test for it, settled by Mitchell on #2032 (2026-10-07). Revision 83 said "`observer_opline_01.phpt` and `observer_fiber_backtrace_crash.phpt` observe opcodes", and read against php-src 8.5.9 neither sets `zend_test.observer.show_opcode_in_user_handler`, the switch that calls `zend_set_user_opcode_handler`: `observer_opline_01.phpt` sets `show_opcode=1`, which prints `EX(opline)->opcode` from the observer's handlers, a frame read (`Z/ext/zend_test/observer.c:64-70`), and `observer_fiber_backtrace_crash.phpt` sets `show_opcode=0`. The one php test that sets it is `ext/opcache/tests/jit/ignored_opcodes.phpt`, so the sentence names it and the check names its program, served without its JIT ini, and the engine's own `crates/php_engine/src/tests/opcodes.rs`, whose sequences are read from the oracle under the `zend-test` variant; revision 83's check had been vacuous for opcodes. The ruling's second half: revision 83 justified the observation by tests that do not use it, and the loop paid a test and a branch per opline for it with nothing declared, where php pays nothing, since it resolves an opline's handler when it compiles it and only an opcode with a user handler resolves to `ZEND_USER_OPCODE`'s (`zend_vm_set_opcode_handler`, `Z/Zend/zend_execute.c:5913-5926`). The declared set is fixed as the engine starts, so the engine chooses there: the loop is generic over whether it tells the declared set's observers, and the engine binds the instantiation that does where an extension declared an opcode and the one with no test where none did, beside the tier it binds, so `execute` and `step` keep one frame layout (ADR-0006). A frame's parameter prologue and a stepping host's `step`, which serve oplines outside the loop, keep their one test of the set. Rejected, by the ruling: removing `ExtensionBuilder::opcodes` until a real extension needs it, and correcting the sentence alone while the loop keeps its per-opline cost. The choice is a second type parameter on the loop beside the tier, which removes the per-opline flag ADR-0006 rejects; ADR-0006 also rejects "a `Mode` trait beside `Tier`" for "two type parameters and four instantiations, one meaningless", and whether that bullet reaches this parameter, every instantiation of which an engine binds, and the ADR takes an amendment line, is #2290's. Check: `grep -n 'observer_opline_01.phpt. and .observer_fiber' third-engine-design.md` finds only this row; with no opcode declared the engine binds the loop whose `before` is empty (`observer::Untold`), as `tests::opcodes` holds; and the bench reading on #2032, beside its A/A control, is the person's to read.

**108. Revision 108 (2026-10-08).** §4.4's *Walking a table* says what a pin keeps, its bytes and nothing else, settled by Mitchell on #2230 (2026-10-07) in the ruling's words: "one pin, and it defers only a block's bytes: the destructor, the rows or members, the object's handle and the memory accounting go at php's moment, for the walk pin and `Obj::pin` alike." Revision 92 typed #2159's pin as a guard "that delays a pinned block's free, and any destructor with it, until the step ends", and #2197 built it so: a row the program unset during its own step was destroyed as the step ended, so output the program wrote in between came first where php, on deterministic input, serves the destructor at the `unset` (#2230's two `serialize` snippets); and a frame's pin of its receiver (`Obj::pin`) served the destructor at php's moment but kept the object's id and its memory until the pin went, so `spl_object_id` and `memory_get_usage` read differently from php's after an autoloader unregistered itself. Now its bytes wait, and only they: `Heap::give_back` is the one end of every block's destruction, retiring the bytes of a pinned block, its free counted where php counts it, and the last pin's let-go gives them back; a block destroyed under a pin stands empty and of its own type, so what the pin names reads it destroyed and never freed, and the cycle collector treats a pin as no owner. Rejected, by the ruling: keeping the pin and settling `trace:pinned-row-destructor`; fixing the walk pin alone and recording `Obj::pin`'s id deferral as a row. A walk that reads the destroyed block where php reads freed memory is the one case left for a divergence row. Nothing an ADR records is reversed, so there is no ADR. Check: the witnesses on `Heap::pin_block` and `Heap::unpin` print what php prints, the destructor before `after unset`, the freed ids reused and the freed memory counted at once.

**109. Revision 109 (2026-10-08).** §4.1 gains the `timelib_sys` row, and §4.1's dependency list and §7.2's date row and last paragraph name the crate as it is built, ruled by Mitchell on #711 (2026-09-28), whose manifest rows he applied in 04c63d6a: the `[[crate]]` row, the `ext/* -> timelib_sys` edge, `timelib_sys` in `unsafe.homes` and `crates/timelib_sys/src/**` in `unsafe.one-op`. #656 had built timelib's binding inside `ext/date`: the vendored `lib/` tree, its `build.rs` and the declarations in `src/ffi/sys.rs`, under the `ext/*/src/ffi/` home, while §7.2 named a `timelib-sys` crate. `crates/timelib_sys` now holds the tree, byte for byte from php-src's `ext/date/lib` at the pinned tag with its `LICENSE.rst`, the `build.rs` that compiles `config0.m4`'s ten sources with its flags, and timelib's C interface declared in Rust, `links = "timelib"`; `ext/date` depends on it and keeps in its `src/ffi/` the safe owned values over it (`Zone`, `Time`, `Tzdb`) and its one `strftime(3)` call. `timelib_time`'s two pointers are private to the crate: they are read through methods, and written through `TimeFields::set_tz_info`, an `unsafe fn`, where the module-private field let any code in `ext/date`'s wrapper write them. The crate carries no lock, as `pcre2-sys` carries none: its one caller is `ext/date`'s `src/ffi/`. Rejected, by the ruling: the binding left in `ext/date` with §7.2 saying so. Nothing an ADR records is reversed, so there is no ADR; ADR-0006's amendment already names `timelib_sys` among the homes. Check: `git ls-files crates/timelib_sys/lib` lists the vendored tree and `git ls-files ext/date/lib ext/date/build.rs` lists nothing; `grep -rn 'extern "C" {' ext/date/src` finds no block of declarations; `cargo xtask unsafe-audit` passes `timelib_sys` as a home; and `cargo xtask api-check` passes `ext/date` with two workspace dependencies.

**110. Revision 110 (2026-10-08).** `php_heap` holds memory and stacks; `php_process` holds the process and its file descriptors, by the rule Mitchell ruled on #1935 (2026-10-07). Revision 80 moved `fork`, the signal pool and `LC_CTYPE` into `php_process` and left four sentences saying what the tree no longer was, and `php_heap` still bound the OS in four places: `process.rs` (`getuid`, `getgid`, `getgroups`, revision 41), `fd.rs` (`poll`, `fcntl`, `fsync`, `dup` and the socket calls, which `php_streams` reaches through `api::net`), `call_stack.rs` and `fiber.rs`. The first two are the process's and move to `php_process` as they stood, with their SAFETY comments; the stacks stay. `heap.lock` loses `process::{uid, gid, groups}`, `select_fds` and the fd calls to `process.lock`, and `api.lock` is unchanged, `api::net` and `api::process` re-exporting the same names from their new crate. §4.1's `php_heap` row reads "the memory model and the stacks", with no claim to the only `unsafe`, and names the call stack's bounds and a fiber's stack; its `php_process` row gains the process identity and its file descriptors; §4.4's process group is bound in `php_process`; §5.8 names `php_process::post` under `php_process`'s SAFETY discipline. §13's closing paragraph and harness-design.md's two counts already cite the `unsafe.homes` rule (revision 106; harness ledger 90), and the rule names crates, so it needs no change. Rejected, by the ruling: the process identity and the fd calls left in `php_heap`, the memory model's crate binding the process after revision 80 moved its other primitives out. Nothing an ADR records is reversed, so there is no ADR. Check: `grep -nE '^(process::|select_fds|poll_fd|bind_fd)' crates/php_heap/heap.lock` finds nothing and `crates/php_process/process.lock` finds each; `git ls-files crates/php_heap/src/fd.rs crates/php_heap/src/process.rs` lists nothing; `cargo xtask unsafe-audit` passes; `grep -n 'php_heap::post\|only .unsafe. in the engine' third-engine-design.md` matches ledger rows alone.

**111. Revision 111 (2026-10-08).** §4.10's list of surfaces is held at the edge by a row a person writes and at the item by a generated lock, ruled by Mitchell on #2020 (2026-10-07) and built by #2251. Revision 31, which made the list, rejected a sentence per surface, each a person's, and the hold of typed names #1826 built made a person the bottleneck again: libxml's surface alone is 393 items, and `xtask/src/api_check/surfaces.rs` kept a list of exports waiting on a row because the hold outran the person. Now the row holds the edge: `api-check` fails a library dependency of one `ext/*` crate on another, dev-dependencies not counted, that no row's Surface cell names with both crates, and the `pdo` row spells its connectors `pdo_mysql`, `pdo_sqlite`. The items are a lock's: each extension's `pub` items beyond `extension()` are `ext/<name>/surface.lock`, regenerated and diffed as `api.lock` is and landed in the same diff as the code, and the typed-name hold goes with its waiting list. The Items and Mirrors cells stay a person's prose, and #2022's two libxml constants need no words, since the lock carries them. The cells ruled the same day land with it: the `standard` → `zlib` row names `bz2`, which calls `fclose` and `fwrite`, and the serializer row names `gmp`, which calls `Unserializer` (#2023); the `random` → `standard` row writes `Rng`, with `default_engine`, `shuffle` and `pick_keys`, beside `php_random.h`'s default engine and `php_array.h`'s and `php_string.h`'s shuffle and pick functions, with the sentence that the edge is inverted from php's (#2021). `Rng::range`, which the ruling made `pub(crate)` for having no caller, is deleted instead, since a `pub(crate)` method with no caller is dead code that clippy denies; `Random\Randomizer::getInt` keeps its own witness. #1363, the items as a region in the design, is withdrawn by the ruling: a region rendered from what the hold reads would hold nothing. Rejected, by the ruling: the typed names, and an edge hold with no record of the items at all. Nothing an ADR records is reversed, so there is no ADR. Check: `cargo xtask api-check` passes every `ext/*` crate, the fixture `xtask/fixtures/api-check/surface-edge` fails on the link no row names and `surface-lock` on the item with no line, and `ls ext/*/surface.lock` lists the thirteen extensions that export a surface.

**112. Revision 112 (2026-10-09).** §5.8 names ext/ffi's deferred callback as a setter of the interrupt, and `Reentry` beside the signal record, settled by Mitchell on #1639 and #1640 (2026-10-03) and built by #1725. #1638 deferred a callback C calls outside a call into C, from a signal handler on the request's thread, to the interrupt check, which made `Reentry::defer` a setter the section did not name. The ruling on #1640 extends it to a callback C calls from another thread while the request runs PHP code: the thread posts the call, here through `Reentry::wait`, and blocks until the request's thread serves it at its next interrupt check and hands back the return, a call still posted at the request's end returning zero; the request is lent to a waiting thread when it enters a call into C and when it blocks in an engine native (`Request::blocking`, which `sleep`, `usleep`, `time_nanosleep`, `time_sleep_until`, `stream_select` and a plain or socket stream's blocking read wait through), so the request never waits on C while C waits on the request. Every output is one php's concurrent run could produce: running the callback at a check is one interleaving of php's race, never calling it is not. The ruling's list was three setters; #2252 had since made an extension's periodic interrupt a setter of its own (#1806), so the list *The interrupt* closes is four, and it names `Reentry::wait` beside `Reentry::defer` because the waited call is posted through it. A lend carries a generation, so the outer lend of a request a callback lent again is not reclaimed until the callback's own lend is. Rejected, by the ruling: a divergence row that leaves the call unserved (#1640's proposal); building a second `&mut Request` for the calling thread (#1614's reverted item 3, undefined behaviour). Nothing an ADR records is reversed, so there is no ADR. Check: the witness on ext/ffi's `serve_deferred` prints `thread` / `loop 4499998500000` / `done` as the oracle does; `ext/ffi/tests/threads.rs` holds a callback posted while the request sleeps in `usleep` served inside the sleep, and one still posted at the request's end returning zero, neither hanging; `php_engine`'s `signal` tests hold the nested lend, the waited call served at the check and on the lent request, and the abandoned one.

**114. Revision 114 (2026-10-09).** §4.4's process group gains the effective ids, `process::euid` and `process::egid`, settled by Mitchell on #1132 (2026-09-28): the process's effective user and group ids are the same engine-owned fact as the real ones (revision 41), bound once in `php_process` beside `uid`, `gid` and `groups`. ext/session's `ps_files_open` ownership rule and ext/posix's `posix_geteuid` and `posix_getegid` read them there, and their own `geteuid(2)`/`getegid(2)` bindings are deleted, ext/session's `src/ffi/` and its `libc` dependency with them.
