A unit's declarations hold their strings as its literals do: an attribute's argument, a backed enum case's value and a static variable's initialiser are the unit's interned strings, as opcache persists them. Under `php -n` php copies them per request, and the difference is the one §5.3 names for literal arrays, `trace:immutable-literal-arrays` (#1472).

### 5.3 Arrays

A port of `zend_hash`, because everything about it is observable: insertion order, `nNextFreeElement` (with the 8.3 negative-key rule), internal pointer semantics under `current`/`next`/`reset`, packed-to-hash conversion points, the rehash that compacts tombstones and moves the internal pointer, and `count()` after `unset`.

```
HashTable  56 bytes      { Refcounted, flags: u32, table_mask: u32, data: *ArData,
                           num_used: u32, num_elements: u32, table_size: u32,
                           internal_pointer: u32, next_free_element: i64, dtor }
Packed:    data = [Zval; table_size]                                  16 B / element
Hash:      data = [u32 hash slots; -table_mask] ++ [Bucket; table_size]   Bucket = { Zval, h: u64, key: *Str } = 32 B
```

Sizes follow Zend (min 8, doubling, `HT_MAX_SIZE`), so `memory_get_usage()` deltas match to the byte for array-heavy tests. Copy-on-write and separation follow `SEPARATE_ARRAY`; `RC` of 1 means unique. References inside arrays are supported exactly as Zend does, including the `IS_ARRAY_IMMUTABLE` duplication rules. Copying a table as php copies it is one rule beside `array_dup`: `zend_array_dup_element`'s unwrapping of a reference with one owner, and `zend_symtable_to_proptable`'s sharing of a table whose keys are all strings. `$GLOBALS`, `get_defined_vars`, the `(object)` cast and the property-table copy use it, and `memory_get_usage()` is held to php's across them. A unit's literal arrays are immutable and shared by every request, as opcache leaves them: a copy adds no reference and a release leaves no GC root. Under the recipe's `php -n`, php refcounts them per request instead, so every observable of a literal's refcount differs from it and agrees with the oracle under opcache: `gc_status()`, `gc_collect_cycles()`, `debug_zval_dump`, `memory_get_usage` over a copied literal and phpdbg's watch report of its old value among them. The difference is the registry's `trace:immutable-literal-arrays` (#917, #1454).

### 5.4 Objects and properties

```
Object     { Refcounted, handle: u32, extra_flags: u32, class: u32, slots: u32, handlers: &'static Payload,
             properties: *HashTable (lazy), native: NativeSlot,
             properties_table: [Zval; slots] }
```

Where php keeps `ce`, the header keeps the class as the engine numbers it and the slot count php reads off `ce->default_properties_count`, the same eight bytes (#1561).

`handlers` is a thin `&'static` payload table beside the class word, eight bytes as php's `zend_object_handlers *` is; the payload the table describes sits after the header. `memory_get_usage()` reads the forty bytes php reads. The 25-slot handler table is reached through the class.

Declared properties live in inline slots at class-determined offsets. Dynamic properties, `get_object_vars`, `foreach` over an object and `(array)` casts materialise the `properties` table with `IS_INDIRECT` entries pointing at the slots. Typed properties, readonly, asymmetric visibility, hooks, lazy objects (ghost and proxy with the 8.4 initialisation triggers), `__get`/`__set` recursion guards, and `WeakMap` keys all attach to this one representation.

**Behaviour is a seam.** Every object carries Zend's 25-slot handler table in 8.5 order (`free_obj`, `dtor_obj`, `clone_obj`, `clone_obj_with`, `read_property`, `write_property`, `read_dimension`, `write_dimension`, `get_property_ptr_ptr`, `has_property`, `unset_property`, `has_dimension`, `unset_dimension`, `get_properties`, `get_method`, `get_constructor`, `get_class_name`, `cast_object`, `count_elements`, `get_debug_info`, `get_closure`, `get_gc`, `do_operation`, `compare`, `get_properties_for`). `Std` implements all of them with the user-class semantics; an internal class overrides the slots it needs and inherits the rest. The kernels call the table; the handlers call the kernels; nothing else knows how an `ArrayObject` indexes.

**An internal class's native payload is one typed slot on the object header, and its tracing is derived, not written.** `php_heap` owns the slot, derives the GC visit and the release from the payload's field types, and exposes the payload to the class's methods as `&Self`/`&mut Self` through the receiver. `get_gc` and `free_obj` are the derived implementations unless a class overrides them, and an override is audited like `unsafe`-adjacent code. No extension body downcasts through `Any` to reach its own state.

**A payload is the class's, and `new` lays it down.** A native class declares its payload type in its header, `#[php_class(payload)]` for the struct itself or `payload = T` for a type several classes share (dom's node, date's `DateObj`), beside its handler table; the declaration reaches `ExtensionBuilder::class_payload::<T>`, and it is php's `ce->create_object`: `new` sizes the object for `T` and writes `T::default()` into the slot before the constructor runs, as `zend_object_alloc(sizeof(custom), ce)` does, so a constructor written in Rust reaches the slot through `PhpObject::native_mut` and no payload is ever handed to an object that was not sized for one. A subclass takes it as it takes the table, since php inherits `create_object`. A method reaches it through its receiver, `This<C>`, whose `payload_mut` answers the payload or the one `InternalError` through `PhpObject::native_mut_or_fatal`; the receiver checks the payload and not `instanceof C`, because an entry reached through `implementation =` serves another class's objects. `create_object` is not a twenty-sixth slot on the handler table: php keeps it on the class entry, and so does this.

Property and method resolution at a call site goes through a **per-opline run-time cache** (a pair of machine words per cache slot, indexed by the opline's `u2`): monomorphic `(class ptr, slot offset)` for fetches, `(class ptr, method ptr)` for calls, `(class ptr, const ptr)` for class constants. On miss, the kernel does the full lookup and refills. The cache is per request through a map pointer (§5.14), so units stay immutable. The object store is a handle table with a free list, which gives `spl_object_id`, weak references, `WeakMap`, and GC enumeration.

### 5.5 References, resources, closures

`Reference { Refcounted, val: Zval, sources: Option<Box<PropSources>> }` with Zend's typed-property reference sources so `$r = &$obj->typedProp; $r = "x";` throws exactly where php throws. Resources are `{ Refcounted, handle: u32, type: ResourceType, ptr }` with a per-type destructor. Closures are objects of the internal `Closure` class with `{ func, this, called_scope, static vars }`; first-class callables, `Closure::fromCallable`, `bind`/`bindTo`/`call`, `getCurrent()` (8.5) and the six 8.5 binding deprecations are properties of that one struct.

### 5.6 Frames and the VM stack

A contiguous stack of 16-byte slots in 256 KiB pages, grown by page and never moved while a frame lives on it. A call frame is a header followed immediately by its slots, laid out `[CVs][TMP/VAR][extra args]`:

```
Frame header  { func: FuncRef, opline: *const Opline, this: Zval, prev: *Frame,
                return_value: *Zval, num_args: u32, call_info: u32,
                symbol_table: Option<*HashTable>, run_time_cache: MapPtr<[Cache]> }
```

Arguments are written by the caller directly into the callee's CV slots (`INIT_FCALL` reserves the frame, `SEND_*` fill it, `DO_FCALL` activates it), so no argument vector is allocated for any call. PHP→PHP calls do not recurse on the Rust stack: the loop switches `frame` and continues. A frame whose op array the request compiled for itself (an `eval`'s, an uncached include's) is served in a scope of the loop's own, one native frame of the loop's body while it stands, so its unit is given back at its return as php's `zend_leave_helper` frees the op array. php's limit moves below each scope by what the scope takes (`StackLimit::nested`), and a frame met with too little of the thread left, or on a fiber's stack, is served without one. Native→PHP re-entry (`__toString`, `usort` callbacks, destructors, autoloaders) recurses via `execute(frame)` on the Rust stack; a stack-limit check (`zend.max_allowed_stack_size` semantics from 8.3) turns a deep recursion into the php-identical `Error` rather than a Rust stack overflow. `$GLOBALS`, `compact`/`extract`, `get_defined_vars`, `include` inside functions and `eval` attach a symbol table to the frame on demand, with CVs exposed through `IS_INDIRECT`.

The native stack is the host's thread's, whatever size the host gave it, and the engine sizes no thread of its own (§5.14): a host whose process serves on its main thread moves its `main` onto a thread `fork::spawn`, the engine's one home, starts at the host's size (`fork::main_thread`), which is the process's main thread to a fork and the only thread a forked child holds, while the thread it started on stands out of every signal's way (`php_process::stand_aside`). `zend.max_allowed_stack_size=0` detects as php detects: `php_heap::call_stack_bounds`, php's `zend_call_stack_get`, asks the platform for the calling thread's base and size, and `php_compile::StackLimit` is built from that less `zend.reserved_stack_size`, as `zend_call_stack_init` builds `EG(stack_limit)`. A thread the platform cannot describe gets php's fallback, the assumed size `zend_call_stack.c` uses on that platform, and the limit is then an estimate as php's is there. A host thread smaller than a program needs is not refused at `serve`: the limit is what the thread has, and a nesting past it is the same fatal php raises on the same thread, earlier.

A request is served on a stack sized so that recursion through a native's callback reaches php's depth on its 8 MiB main thread: the CLI serves its request on a thread of that size started through `fork::spawn`, and makala's `REQUEST_STACK` is the same size, reserved and committed only as touched. When the tail-call dispatch of §5.8 (revision 147) gives the loop a dispatcher's frame, the size comes down with what a level costs (#3043). The size is stated once, `php_sapi::REQUEST_STACK`, which the CLI's thread and makala's request threads both read.

Under detect, a native's own recursion and the compiler are held to php's budget, the main thread's size less `zend.reserved_stack_size`, counted from the stack's base or, inside a native's call back into PHP and a generator's resume, from the frame that entered the loop, and never past the floor; `execute`'s entry is held to the floor. So the engine never refuses a recursion php serves, and the `Error` names php's budget whichever check refused. A size `zend.max_allowed_stack_size` gives is one limit for both (#3142).

**The limit and the floor** (revision 43). `StackLimit` carries two addresses: the limit, `EG(stack_limit)`, and the floor, the thread's own end with `zend.reserved_stack_size` above it. A walk php guards asks the limit and raises php's refusal; a walk php leaves to the machine asks the floor and raises `zend_call_stack_size_error`'s `Error` one reserve short of the thread's end. So a nest php takes whole under a small `zend.max_allowed_stack_size` is taken whole here, and the one php faults on is a catchable `Error`, the divergence `trace:stack-floor-refusal`.

**In `php_heap`.** A frame is raw memory with an invariant ("never moves while live"), which is `unsafe` territory. `php_heap::VmStack` owns the pages and exposes `Frame` with typed, bounds-checked slot cells: `frame.slot(Slot) -> Zval`, `frame.set(Slot, Zval)`, `frame.slot_ptr(Slot) -> ZvalRef` for `VAR`/`IS_INDIRECT` cases, plus `push_frame(func, num_args)`, `pop_frame`, `move_to_heap` (generators). The run-time cache and the map-pointer table are also `php_heap` storage. `php_engine` stays `#![forbid(unsafe_code)]`.

**A chain another process can read** (revision 59, revision 142, revision 143). Every push, a user frame and a native call alike, writes the room the VM stack reserves before the frame's slots: its first word is the stack's link, the previous room's address, and the 72 bytes after it are a `#[repr(C)]` header whose first word names what the frame serves, php's `EX(func)`, as the address of a record holding the callee's name and, for user code, the frame's file and oplines, each as address and length into the unit and the linked tables; after it the opline the frame last saved, by its place, from which a reader takes the line as php reads `opline->lineno`, and the frame's flags, which carry its kind, php's `ZEND_CALL_TOP_CODE` among them. The request keeps the innermost room's address in one word, moved only after the room it names is complete, so a process stopped between any two instructions holds a chain another process can walk as `fpm_php_trace_dump` walks `prev_execute_data`, reading bytes and never Rust values; a call still being built and a frame being popped are on it, and their flags tell the reader to pass them, as php's chain does not hold them. A fiber's first room links to its resumer's innermost at each `zend_fiber_resume`, as php writes `fiber->stack_bottom->prev_execute_data`. A generator resumed through a `yield from` links the frame it runs to a room the generator it resumed holds, off the VM stack's pages, which serves nothing and links the resumer's innermost, as php links the running frame to `orig_generator->execute_fake` (`Z/Zend/zend_generators.c:807`), so another process reads `???` there. `serve` hands the host's `look` the word's address, which stands from the request's start to its end. The header is written at the one push site and always, so it cannot disagree with the engine's own frame state by mode; a native call's header takes room on the VM stack as php's internal call frame does; an unobserved frameless call, which php serves over no frame, takes none, and its record is on the chain a backtrace walks, where php adds a frame for the call (`Z/Zend/zend_builtin_functions.c:1984`), and never on the chain another process reads, which `fpm_php_trace_dump` walks along `prev_execute_data` and which php's call is not on (#2553).

**The entry slot.** `VmStack` has three `heap.lock` items for the tier: `entry() -> FrameRef`, the entry frame of the innermost `execute` on this stack; `push_entry(f) -> EntryGuard`, pushed by `execute` on entry, popped by the guard's drop so that an `Err(Bailout)` pops too, because shutdown functions and destructors run `execute` again after a bailout; and `current() -> FrameRef`, the frame the loop is executing. The frame header gains no bit: "this is the entry frame of an `execute`" is a fact about the stack and not about the frame. A generator resumed by a nested `execute` is a heap frame; the slot holds its `FrameRef`, which the running resume keeps alive. The slot is per fiber because the stack is (§5.11).

**Activation.** The `DO_UCALL` and `DO_FCALL*` handlers activate a user frame and so does `execute` for its own entry frame: push, position `frame.opline` as php's `i_init_func_execute_data` does, skipping the whole `RECV*` run when the function has no typed parameters and was passed no more arguments than it declares, and starting at opline zero otherwise (`Z/Zend/zend_execute.c:4426-4437`), run the remaining `RECV*` handlers in a loop bounded by `OpArray.safepoints[0]`, then, if none raised, perform the interrupt check (§5.8) and call `T::safepoint(req, callee, Site::Entry)`, and continue at the first body opline. If a `RECV*` raised, the exception is pending and `Entry` is not raised. php's counter sits after the `RECV*` run for an untyped function and on opline zero for a typed one (`Z/ext/opcache/jit/zend_jit_trace.c:8983-9001`), so for a typed function php counts a call whose `RECV` throws and this engine does not; that difference in trace roots is an accepted `trace` row of the divergence registry, checked by the `TRACE start` diff (§8), and is kept because a `RECV` that throws must never be inside a recording (`jit-design.md` §4.1). A generator frame is activated the same way before `GENERATOR_CREATE` moves it and is not activated again on resume. The comparison per prologue opline is paid once per call and never per body opline; a function with no parameters has an empty prologue and one compare.

### 5.7 Op arrays and the op spec

An `Opline` is php's `zend_op`, field for field and thirty-two bytes: `{ opcode: u8, op1_type: u8, op2_type: u8, result_type: u8, op1: u32, op2: u32, result: u32, extended_value: u32, lineno: u32, handler: Handler }`, aligned to thirty-two so an opline's place is its index shifted. `handler` is php's `zend_op.handler`, a number where php's is a pointer: one `php_unit::Handler` per opcode and per combination its `SPEC(...)` specialises on the hot list (the operands' kinds, `SMART_BRANCH`, `OP_DATA`, and `RETVAL` beside the kinds, revision 159), resolved from the opline and the one after it where the unit is published (`Unit::published`), as `pass_two` calls `zend_vm_set_opcode_handler`, and `Unresolved` until then. Publication makes a type of its own, `php_unit::PublishedUnit`, which `Unit::published` alone constructs and through which every frame's op array is read, a file's frame holding one and a function's frame reading it through the function table, so `execute` enters no op array an opline of which is `Unresolved`; the dispatch's `Unresolved` arm stays, unreachable by construction, because `Unresolved` stays the value before publication. `opcode` is one byte because php's numbers are, and `result_type` is the byte that buys: the dump opens a line with the result operand when it is `TMP`, `VAR` or `CV`, `DO_ICALL` and `ASSIGN` read it as `RETURN_VALUE_USED`, and the compiler writes `UNUSED` there where `zend_do_free` would. A site opcode's `result` stays the safepoint number and its `result_type` stays `UNUSED`, as php leaves it.

```
Operand kinds        CONST (literal index) | TMP | VAR | CV (slot index) | UNUSED
OpArray             { oplines: Box<[Opline]>, literals: Box<[Zval]> (immutable), vars: Box<[&Str]>,
                      num_cvs, num_tmps, try_catch: Box<[TryCatch]>, live_ranges: Box<[LiveRange]>,
                      safepoints: Box<[OplineIdx]>,
                      static_vars, arg_info, attributes, doc_comment, filename, line_start/end, cache_size }
```

**The instruction set is php's, exactly, as `zend_compile.c` and `pass_two` leave it.** Before the optimizer there are no JIT-only opcodes: `Z/Zend/zend_vm_def.h` has 207 handlers, one inline handler (`RETURN`), 42 type-specialised handlers and 34 helpers (`ops.toml` `[meta]`), and every opcode the compiler emits is in the pre-optimiser dump (§6.1). `pass_two` is part of the emission: it rewrites `BRK` and `CONT` to `JMP` and `RETURN*` inside a generator to `GENERATOR_RETURN` (`Z/Zend/zend_opcode.c:1154-1165`, `:1213-1217`), so this compiler emits what `pass_two` leaves. Opcodes only the optimizer introduces are also implemented so that an optimizer, if one is ever built (§6.1), needs no new handlers. The set is written once in `ops.toml`: name, operand kinds accepted, result kind, **effects** (`throw`, `alloc`, `call`, `write_globals`), specialisation list, **`safepoint`**, and a doc block. The `safepoint` column names per opcode the field that carries the safepoint number: `result`, on the backward jumps `pass_two` leaves, `JMPNZ` (`while`, `do`-`while`, `for`) and `JMP` (a `foreach`'s loop end, a `continue` inside a `foreach`, a backward `goto`), on `JMPZ`, which jumps backward only after the optimizer's `INV_COND` (`Z/Zend/Optimizer/block_pass.c:699-707`; `zend_emit_cond_jump` does not invert on `BOOL_NOT`, so `do { } while (!$x)` is `BOOL_NOT` then a backward `JMPNZ`), and on `RETURN`, `RETURN_BY_REF` and `GENERATOR_RETURN`. All six leave `result` unused in every handler body, transitively through the helpers, and every emission passes a null result (`data/ops-extraction.md`); `RETURN_BY_REF` reads `extended_value` and `RETURN` sets it to `-1` on the implicit final return, which is why `result` is the field. `JMPZ_EX`, `JMPNZ_EX`, `JMP_NULL`, `JMP_SET`, `COALESCE`, `JMP_FRAMELESS`, `FE_RESET_*`, `FE_FETCH_*`, `SWITCH_*`, `MATCH` and `CATCH` jump forward and are not sites. `FAST_RET` jumps to `fast_call + 1`, an earlier index, and is not a back-edge: it is the `finally` subroutine's return with no CFG successor (`Z/Zend/zend_cfg.c:508`). The generated dispatch passes the field's value on `Site` from the handler that has it. That a site opcode leaves `result` unused is a build error, not a runtime one, if wrong; that every backward jump other than `FAST_RET` has a column and a numbered header is the compiler's assertion (§6.1).

`ops.toml` is consumed four times (the opcode enum, operand kinds, effects, `may_throw`, `safepoint` and the disassembler generated in `php_unit`'s `build.rs`; the dispatch `match` generated in `php_engine`'s `build.rs` over `php_unit::Handler`; the compiler's emission; xtask's disassembly) and is therefore locked as `ops.lock`. One enum, so the compiler, the dump and the dispatch cannot disagree on a number.

### 5.8 Dispatch

A `loop { match handler }` over the dense `u16` each opline was resolved to at publication, with handlers as `#[inline(always)]` functions. Per-op overhead is limited to: fetch opline, switch. There is **no** per-op pending check (only after ops whose spec says `throw`, generated), **no** per-op safepoint, **no** profiling counter, **no** destructor-queue check, and, in an engine where no extension declared an opcode, **no** test for observed opcodes: the loop is generic over whether it tells them, and the engine binds the instantiation as it starts (§7.1, revision 107).

**Bodies written once, monomorphised over operand kinds.** The hot opcodes are these:

<!-- computed exec cmd=scripts/specialised-ops.sh inputs=ops.toml,scripts/specialised-ops.sh name=specialised | do not edit; run computed -->

`ADD`, `SUB`, `MUL`, `CONCAT`, `IS_IDENTICAL`, `IS_EQUAL`, `IS_SMALLER`, `ASSIGN`, `ASSIGN_DIM`, `ASSIGN_OBJ`, `ASSIGN_OP`, `ASSIGN_DIM_OP`, `PRE_INC`, `PRE_DEC`, `JMPZ`, `JMPNZ`, `FAST_CONCAT`, `ROPE_INIT`, `ROPE_ADD`, `ROPE_END`, `INIT_FCALL`, `RETURN`, `SEND_VAL`, `FE_FETCH_R`, `FETCH_DIM_R`, `FETCH_OBJ_R`, `FETCH_DIM_W`, `FETCH_OBJ_W`, `FETCH_DIM_RW`, `FETCH_DIM_FUNC_ARG`, `ISSET_ISEMPTY_DIM_OBJ`, `SEND_VAR`, `DO_ICALL`, `DO_UCALL`, `COUNT`

<!-- /computed in=bf0516b8c4a78363e936726b0aa66f3d3968231003d851ada60d8f311d0e5798 out=d90f8536d5d7caa8465435e60c1f734fc31c90f745bd0c0a737d13bf5053745c -->

Which opcodes are specialised is `ops.toml`'s `specialise`, and the list above is rendered from it. An opcode php's `zend_vm_gen.php` specialises on its operand kinds joins in the commit that measures, by instructions retired on a kernel under the protocol, its run-time operand decoding spending what php's specialised handler does not; that commit carries the measurement, and no ruling is asked for (#2997).

For each, `build.rs` reads the specialisation list and emits `match` arms `ADD_CV_CONST => add::<Cv, Const>(..)`, and so on:

```rust
#[inline(always)]
fn add<A: Operand, B: Operand>(cx: &mut Request, f: &Frame, op: &Opline) -> Next {
    let a = A::read(f, op.op1);            // static offset for CV/TMP/VAR, literal for CONST
    let b = B::read(f, op.op2);
    if let (Some(x), Some(y)) = (a.as_long(), b.as_long()) { /* inline fast path */ }
    else if let (Some(x), Some(y)) = (a.as_double(), b.as_double()) { /* inline */ }
    else { return ops::add_slow(cx, f, op, a, b) }  // cold kernel, may throw
    ...
}
```

A body that disagrees with the spec is a compile error. This is `zend_vm_gen.php` done by the Rust compiler; ≈ 30 hot opcodes × ≤ 16 operand pairs plus ≈ 180 unspecialised handlers is ≈ 650 arms. Whether `#[inline(always)]` and the dense `match` reach a jump table is measured under the protocol, not assumed.

**The handler contains no semantics.** A handler does operand addressing, the inline `(LONG, LONG)`/`(DOUBLE, DOUBLE)` fast path that Zend's `fast_add_function` also inlines, and a call to a kernel in `php_engine::ops`. The kernel is the unit that carries a witness (§8). Because the same kernel is what `api` exposes to natives and what the tier's `kernels!` list wraps, one semantic lives in one function and every caller gets it; no `*Step` protocol exists between them.

**`SMART_BRANCH` is a handler property, not a compiler one.** php's pre-optimiser dump for `if ($v < 2) continue;` is `IS_SMALLER` followed by a separate `JMPZ` (`research/data/r2-php-oparray-dump.txt`, oplines 0014–0015); the fusion is the comparison handler's, as php's is: a comparison whose line says `SMART_BRANCH` is resolved where its unit is published to its `_JMPZ` or `_JMPNZ` handler, and to the one whose jump goes back or the one whose jump goes forward (revision 160), which reads the jump for its target and safepoint number alone, and every other comparison tests the opline after it at run time, where php's `ZEND_VM_SMART_BRANCH` reads the mark `zend_emit_cond_jump` left in its `result_type` and `pass_two` picked its handler from. The op array is not changed, so op-array identity holds. **A fused handler is one handler over two oplines**, and that is the rule everywhere the loop is observed: `step` runs it as one step and reports `Same` at the opline after both; a backward fused branch raises `Site::Loop` from the fused handler, which reads the number from the branch opline's field; the tier's recorder records one event with the direction taken; and the tier's exit for a mispredicted fused branch lands on the branch opline with the comparison's result written as a constant (`jit-design.md` §4.1, §4.2).

**`execute` is `execute::<T: Tier>`**, instantiated once per tier a binary binds, `Nop` always, `php_jit::Jit` in a `jit` build and the debugger in the `phpdbg` binary, and its loop body returns the edge it took so that `step` can hand it out. **The debugging host is a `Tier` too:** its `safepoint` is the gate and `step` is its loop, as the recorder's (`jit-design.md` §2.4, revision 51), so phpdbg observes every opline, its breakpoints and its watchpoints with no second loop and no test on the interpreter's path. The interpreter's own adapter, `Nop`, makes the safepoint a folded branch. The safepoints are in the backward `JMP*` handlers after operand reads and before the jump, in `RETURN*` after operand reads and before the pop, and in the activation of a frame (§5.6). `Site::Return` is never raised for the entry frame of an `execute`, which the handler knows from the stack's entry slot.

**The interrupt.** `Limits::interrupt` is one `AtomicBool`, set by the engine's execution timer when `max_execution_time` elapses, by `pcntl`'s asynchronous signal delivery, by an extension's periodic interrupt (`Limits::start_periodic`, #1806), and by ext/ffi's deferred callback through `api::request::Reentry::defer` (and `Reentry::wait`, for a callback C calls from another thread), and by nothing else; setting it is all any of them does. The loop tests it at four sites, and where compiled code hands it the check, and nowhere else: at a back-edge `JMP*`; at frame activation, which is `ZEND_VM_INTERRUPT_CHECK` at `ZEND_VM_ENTER_EX`; after every `Flow::Resume`; and after an internal call returns, which is php's `ZEND_VM_FCALL_INTERRUPT_CHECK` (`Z/Zend/zend_execute.c:4318`) in the loop's `DO_ICALL`, `DO_FCALL_BY_NAME` and `DO_FCALL` handlers (`Z/Zend/zend_vm_def.h:4141`, `:4270`, `:4398`), and the same check in `call_function` after a native that a native called, which is `zend_call_function`'s (`Z/Zend/zend_execute_API.c:1051-1058`), with the call still on the stack in both, so a signal a native sent its own process is dispatched before the next opline. A native that threw leaves the interrupt raised for the next site, since php calls no PHP handler while `EG(exception)` stands. When set, the interrupt helper clears it and either raises the timeout fatal as a `Bailout` with php's text or dispatches the pending signals to their PHP handlers, which is `zend_interrupt_function`. Compiled code tests the same flag once per loop iteration (`jit-design.md` §4.3) and hands it to the loop, never answering it itself: by an exit, php's `ZEND_JIT_EXIT_TO_VM`, or, where php's trace takes its interrupt stub (`jit_stub_interrupt_handler`, `Z/ext/opcache/jit/zend_jit_trace.c:7210-7228`), by answering `INTERRUPTED`, at which the loop makes the check through `tier::interrupted` and enters the trace again (#3137). The flag is a `tier.lock` item. php tests the flag in one more place than this loop does: `ZEND_VM_SET_OPCODE` is `OPLINE = new_op; ZEND_VM_INTERRUPT_CHECK()` (`Z/Zend/zend_execute.c:5770-5787`), so every jump that sets an opline tests `EG(vm_interrupt)`, a taken forward jump included. Inside a loop that holds a forward jump beside the jump back php therefore names whichever opline the interrupt fell on, and is measured naming both: `set_time_limit(1); $x = 0; for (;;) if ($x) ; else` with the block on line 3 times out on line 3 on ten runs of the pinned binary at 8.5.9 and on line 2 on the other two of twelve, where this engine, testing at the back-edge alone, names line 2 on all twelve, the line of the opline the jump back lands on. The op arrays are byte-identical (§6.1); only the observation point differs, and the line such a timeout names is not held.

**Where a signal is recorded** (revision 38, revision 110). A signal's disposition is the process's, so what its handler records is the process's too, split three ways by role. `php_process::post` holds the mechanism and knows no signal: a fixed pool of slots generic over a `Copy` record, which a writer interrupting anything fills by a compare-and-swap without allocating or locking (`post(fill)`) and a safe context takes by a predicate in the order the records arrived (`take(want, each)`), and a wake set that raises every interrupt registered on it; its raw pointers and `unsafe` are under `php_process`'s SAFETY discipline. `php_engine` holds the policy with no `unsafe`: the one static pool of `Siginfo` records and the one wake set, which numbers a request takes (the ones its handler table holds), and a request's interrupt on the wake set while `pcntl_async_signals(true)` stands; it runs at the interrupt check or a dispatch the program called, never in signal context. `ext/pcntl`'s `src/ffi/` holds the OS boundary, beside `nix`'s `sigaction`: the `extern "C"` handler and the `siginfo_t` decoding, which is all that runs in signal context with the pool's post and the wake beside it. The api's face is the post alone, so an extension can write a record and raise, and taking is the engine's `take_signals`. Beside the static `Siginfo` pool and wake set is `Reentry`, one per request whose FFI made a callback (`crates/php_engine/src/signal.rs`): a lent-address word, a pool of `Deferred` calls, and the slots of the calls a thread other than the request's waits on.

**Tail-call dispatch** (revision 147, #2970). Rust's explicit tail calls (`become`, the `explicit_tail_calls` feature) and the `rust-preserve-none` calling convention (`rust_preserve_none_cc`) are gated on every toolchain, nightly included, and the first is not accepted as an RFC. The `match` loop is shipped on the pinned stable toolchain. The threaded variant, `cfg(dispatch = "tailcall")` (#2905), is held by the threaded dispatch's lane on a dated nightly (`harness-design.md` §7) and shipped by no build. The decision is taken again when `explicit_tail_calls` and `rust_preserve_none_cc` are stable, on wall-clock readings under the protocol; nothing here assumes the variant is faster.

### 5.9 Errors, exceptions, fatals, exit

Three distinct mechanisms, as in Zend. **Exceptions** are pending state (`EG(exception)`). An op with the `throw` effect is followed by a generated check that jumps to the `HANDLE_EXCEPTION` handler, which walks the op array's try/catch table, runs `finally` blocks through `FAST_CALL`/`FAST_RET`, unwinds frames, and re-raises across native re-entry boundaries by returning to the nested `execute` caller. **Fatals and `exit`** are `Bailout`, an `Err` carried by `execute() -> Result<(), Bailout>` and propagated with `?` only through the nested-execute recursion; `zend_bailout` without `longjmp`. Uncaught exceptions convert to a fatal at the request boundary with php's exact `Fatal error: Uncaught …` / `Stack trace:` rendering, including 8.5's `fatal_error_backtraces`. **Warnings, notices, deprecations** go through one `php_error_docref`-shaped function: level, message id, formatted text, file, line, then `error_reporting`, `@` scopes, `display_errors`, `log_errors`, `html_errors`, and user handlers. Message text is a table indexed by id, the 85 php-exact wordings from Mahout as data and the rest from php-src. Two witnesses per message, minimum.

### 5.10 Destructors, shutdown

`zval_ptr_dtor` semantics: when a refcount reaches zero the destructor runs **synchronously**, on the spot, by nested `execute`. Object destruction during GC follows `zend_gc`'s ordering. Shutdown order is `php_request_shutdown`'s: shutdown functions, `__destruct` on the symbol table's objects in reverse, repeated while the count of symbols moves, then on the object store in handle order, output flushed, output handlers finalised, `ob` stack, extensions' `rshutdown` in reverse startup order, and the symbol table destroyed after them, `zend_hash_graceful_reverse_destroy` in `zend_shutdown_executor_values`. Mahout's twenty registered dtor/GC-order divergences are the argument for doing this the way Zend does rather than with a queue.

**How the heap runs a destructor without knowing the VM.** `php_heap` depends on nothing, yet a refcount reaching zero inside a hash-table clear must run `__destruct`. Every heap operation that can free an object or resource takes `hooks: &mut dyn HeapHooks` (seam 1). The operation reaches the heap through the hooks as well: `hooks.heap()` is asked for again after every call up, because a call up may run PHP code that allocates, and a `&mut Heap` held across it would be a second exclusive borrow of the request. php has no such step because `EG(objects_store)` is a global; here the request owns its heap, and the one form Rust admits is the heap handed back each time it is needed. `Request` implements the hooks by calling `execute` for `destruct`, dispatching `free_obj`/`get_gc` to the object's handler table, and returning a `Fatal` for `out_of_memory`. Heap operations that cannot free anything do not take the parameter, so the signature says which operations may re-enter. Under Miri the heap's tests pass a `Recorder` adapter that logs `(handle, event)` instead of running anything, which is how the ordering rules of `zend_gc` are tested without a VM.

### 5.11 Generators and fibers

Generators are heap frames: on first `yield`, the frame's header and slots move from the VM stack into a `Box<[Zval]>` owned by the `Generator` object through `VmStack::move_to_heap`; resumption pushes a delegating frame. `yield from`, keys, `send`, `throw`, `getReturn`, `finally` on destruction, and generator-in-generator delegation trees follow `zend_generators.c`.

Fibers are coroutines: each `Fiber` owns a machine stack of its own and its own VM stack pages, and `Fiber::start`/`resume`/`suspend` switch both. The whole nested execution, native frames included, lives on the fiber's stack, which is the model Zend uses.

**The switch is an assembly context switch.** php's is boost.context's `jump_fcontext`, reached through `zend_fiber_switch_context` (`Z/Zend/zend_fibers.c:476`): the callee-saved registers and the stack pointer and nothing else. `swapcontext` is the `ZEND_FIBER_UCONTEXT` arm php's default build does not compile, and no thread is parked per fiber. This engine's switch is `corosensei`'s, the same switch written in Rust for the targets §4.1 names, over a stack laid down as php lays one down: `fiber.stack_size` rounded up to a page with one guard page below it (`ZEND_FIBER_GUARD_PAGES`, `:206-280`), and php's `ZEND_FIBER_DEFAULT_C_STACK_SIZE`, 2 MiB on a 64-bit target, where the ini entry is unset (`Z/Zend/zend.c:244-249`). A thread per fiber is not the mechanism and could not be: a request runs to completion on one thread (§5.14), and `Fiber::suspend` is reached from inside a native, a destructor or a kernel, which has to return to the resumer with the request still one thread's.

**What the switch swaps is the engine's execution state, enumerated.** php's list is `zend_fiber_vm_state` (`:105-155`), eleven fields captured before the jump and restored after it: the VM stack with its top, end and page size; the current frame; `error_reporting`; the tier's trace number; the bailout target; the active fiber; and, under `ZEND_CHECK_STACK_LIMIT`, the stack base and limit. Here they are one value on the `Fiber` object under this engine's names: the `Vm` the request is running on, which owns the `VmStack` and with it every frame's header, the current frame and the entry slot of §5.6, all facts about a stack; the error-reporting level; the trace number, `tier::TraceNum`; and the `StackLimit`. What a frame owns beyond its header's words stands in the request's slab of annexes and is not swapped, since a generator's frame keeps its annex as it moves between stacks. Seam 9's chain of open observed frames is swapped with them, as php swaps `EG(current_observed_frame)` at the switch's notice (`Z/Zend/zend_observer.c:423-424`): its frames are facts about the stack they stand on, as the current frame is. The rule that list is an instance of, and the one the section states: **a switch swaps the engine's execution state and nothing an extension or the tier owns.** php's tier sits on the engine's side of that line — `jit_trace_num` is a field of Zend's executor globals, so the switch saves it and `zend_fiber_execute` zeroes it for the fiber's body (`:599`), as `zend_generators.c:793-865` does around a resume — and so does this engine's (revision 52, #627): the trace number, which numbers the trace executing, is one word the engine stores per context and the tier alone interprets, so the switch saves it with the rest, a fiber's body starts at zero, and a generator resume does the same. It excludes nothing: the tier's recording exclusion is the request's, as php's `JIT_G(tracing)` is, the tier's own request state that no switch saves (`jit-design.md` §4.9; revision 153, #2999). The word is engine storage lent to the tier, so the rule stands: the switch swaps the engine's execution state.

**A fiber's VM stack pages are php's fiber pages, not the request's.** `zend_fiber_execute` gives the fiber one fresh page of `ZEND_FIBER_VM_STACK_SIZE`, 1,024 slots and 16 KiB, and makes that the page size for everything the fiber grows (`:584-588`), where the request's own stack grows in the 256 KiB pages of §5.6. `memory_get_usage()` reads the difference, so the page size inside a fiber is the fiber's.

**The call-stack limit inside a fiber is the fiber's stack.** `zend.max_allowed_stack_size=0` does not detect the thread's bounds on a fiber: `zend_fiber_execute` writes the base and the limit from the fiber's own stack, `zend_fiber_stack_base` and `zend_fiber_stack_limit` (`:337-359`), the limit the stack's end plus `zend.reserved_stack_size`, doubled on Apple for `___chkstk_darwin`'s probe, and php says in as many words that the maximum for a fiber is `fiber.stack_size` (`Z/Zend/zend.c:276`). So `php_heap::call_stack_bounds` is asked once for the thread (§5.6) and never for a fiber, whose `php_compile::StackLimit` is built from the stack the engine allocated for it — the one stack the engine does size, which is what §12's row says — and a recursion deep enough inside a fiber raises php's `Error` at `fiber.stack_size` rather than at the thread's.

**The interrupt is not swapped.** `EG(vm_interrupt)` is not a field of `zend_fiber_vm_state`; it is the request's, and so is `Limits::interrupt` (§5.8). A timer that fires while a fiber runs is read by that fiber at its next back-edge, frame activation, `Resume` or return from an internal call, whichever comes first, and the timeout fatal is raised there, on the fiber's stack. It leaves as a `Bailout`: php catches it at the fiber's own `zend_first_try`, marks the transfer `ZEND_FIBER_TRANSFER_FLAG_BAILOUT` and re-raises `zend_bailout()` on the resumer (`:627-629`, `:669-674`); here `execute` on the fiber returns `Err(Bailout)`, the switch carries it, and `Fiber::resume` returns it on the resumer's stack to be propagated with `?` as every other bailout is (§5.9). One fiber's timeout is the request's.

**A frame off the main stack is a frame.** The engine has no second word for it and needs none: which fiber a frame belongs to is a fact about the stack it is on, as "this is the entry frame of an `execute`" already is (§5.6), so the frame header gains no bit and `Edge` gains no arm. A fiber switch is also not a step: it happens inside `Fiber::suspend` or `Fiber::resume`, a native that the loop reports as `Same` like any other, so the recorder never sees a frame chain change under it and never records across a stack. Because fibers own their VM stack pages, the entry slot of §5.6 switches with the stack, and no save-and-restore of "the entry frame" is written anywhere: two fibers are two `execute` nestings and two slots. The same holds for compiled code: a trace's machine frame suspended inside a kernel waits on its fiber's stack, which is why the crossing's raw-pointer invariant is stated per fiber stack (`jit-design.md` §4.6).

Unwinding a fiber on destruction throws `FiberError`/`GracefulExit` through its frames exactly as php does. GC walks suspended fibers' and generators' frames as roots.

### 5.12 Cycle collection

A port of `zend_gc.c`: purple root buffer, `gc_possible_root` called at precisely the sites Zend calls it, threshold 10,000 with Zend's adaptive growth and shrink rules, mark-grey/scan/scan-black/collect-white, destructor phase with resurrection detection, `gc_status()` fields including `runs`, `collected`, `threshold`, `buffer_size`, `application_time`/`collector_time`, and the 8.5 change to `gc_collect_cycles()`'s return value. `gc_children` and the destructor phase go through `HeapHooks`. Mahout's R169–R174 and R191–R195 were root-buffer population differences; they are avoided only by placing the calls where Zend places them.

### 5.13 Allocator and memory accounting

`php_heap::mm` is a `zend_alloc` port: 2 MiB chunks, 4 KiB pages, the 30 small-size bins, large runs, huge allocations via `mmap`, per-request ownership, mass release at request end. All PHP-visible heap objects allocate through it. `memory_get_usage(false)` is the bin accounting; `memory_get_usage(true)` is chunk count × 2 MiB; `memory_limit` is enforced on chunk growth with php's exact message. **The allocator is the only accountant**: a string, array, object or frame php allocates cannot exist without passing through `mm`, and no `api` item lets a native tell the engine about memory. The one record that stands off it is an unobserved frameless call's, which php does not lay down at all: `php_heap` keeps it in a reused room, one per such call in flight, never larger than a header and three operands, so it takes no room php's call does not (#2553). **At php's sizes** (revision 50, #1170). What `memory_get_usage()` and `memory_limit` observe is what php's `emalloc` is asked for, so a structure php allocates on its request heap is allocated on `mm` at the size php asks for. An object takes `zend_object_alloc(sizeof(custom), ce)`, its native payload laid inside that allocation, with a `const` assertion that the Rust type fits. A closure takes `sizeof(zend_closure)`, with its static variables duplicated on `mm` as `zend_array_dup` duplicates them and its run-time cache allocated where `zend_create_closure_ex` allocates one. A generator's frame is copied into an allocation of its own, as `GENERATOR_CREATE` copies it. Bytes a payload or a request state keeps where php keeps them in `emalloc`'d memory are held in the request heap's own buffer, which grows as `smart_str` grows. What a C library allocates through its own allocator stays with the compiler and the syntax tree as the recorded approximation. Each payload class carries a witness of its `new` delta. The compiler's temporaries and the syntax tree on the system allocator remain the one recorded approximation; the tier's compile allocations, on the compile worker, do not count against the recording request's `memory_limit`, as php's JIT buffer does not (unverified). A request's memory at its first line is opcache's: what php allocates there whatever it compiles, the compiler's arena among it with the run-time caches zend_arena_alloc carves from it, is on mm at php's sizes, and the main script's compile is charged as an include's is (§6.3), by opcache's lookup. So a witness of a request's starting usage, or of a memory_limit fatal's requested size, names [regime.jit-off] (#2863).

### 5.14 Request lifecycle, threads, and map pointers

`Engine` (process-wide, `Sync`): interned string table, unit cache of `Published` values, linked immutable classes, ini defaults, every extension's engine state, the tier's engine state. `Request` (`Send`, not `Sync`): allocator, object store, globals, constants, ini overrides, output buffers, error state, the interrupt flag, per-request caches, every extension's request state, `rinit`/`rshutdown`.

**Threads.** A host runs each request to completion on one thread over one `Engine`. Whether the host spawns a thread per request or keeps a bounded pool is the host's choice and is invisible to the engine, because no engine or extension state is thread-local: state that outlives a request lives in the engine (§4.4), state that does not lives in the request, and the request leak gate is what a pooled thread would otherwise have leaked into. This is opcache's memory model applied to threads instead of processes; ADR-0005's argument stands on the absence of thread-local state, not on the absence of a pool. `catch_unwind` at the request boundary converts any panic into a request failure with a logged internal error and a clean `Request` drop; the thread and process survive. **The engine owns every thread it runs, and joins each one before a fork and at engine end;** an extension owns no thread (ADR-0010). There are two that are not a request. One execution-timer thread per engine serves every request's deadline and periodic interrupt (#2252). And under `jit.compile=thread`, the default, the tier's engine state owns one compile worker per `Engine` that runs the pipeline from a trace record to a published trace; recording stays on the request thread that holds the frame, and everything after the record is pure over owned values. `jit.compile=inline` runs the pipeline on the request that won the claim, which `jit.mode=force` and `exit-fuzz` imply, so every per-commit test is deterministic. The worker touches no request, no frame and no map pointer. A C library whose allocator macros take no context (timelib and lexbor, and later libmbfl) allocates on the running request's heap through `php_heap`'s lend: a guard that holds the request's heap in a per-thread slot for exactly one call into the library and clears it on return, so nothing outlives the call; outside a lend the shim's `emalloc` falls back to the system allocator. A thread-local heap that outlives a call stays rejected (revision 50).

**Serve.** The lifecycle is one function: take a request description and a sink, compile or fetch the unit, seed superglobals and GPC from the description, run, commit headers at the first body byte or at shutdown, run shutdown functions and destructors in `php_request_shutdown` order, finalise output handlers, report observables (exit code, whether headers were sent, the rendered fatal if any). No host contains any of these steps. Stdin is a stream on the description and is read only when the script reads it. The embed API is `Engine::serve` and nothing else.

**Map pointers.** An immutable, shared class still has per-request mutable state: static properties, class constants and property defaults with constant expressions evaluated lazily, each op array's run-time cache and `static` variables, and each extension's request state. Zend solves all of them with one mechanism, `ZEND_MAP_PTR`. Here `MapPtr<T>` is a newtype over an offset assigned by `Engine` when a unit, class or extension is published; `Request` owns a `MapTable` (in `php_heap`) and `req.map(ptr) -> &mut T` allocates on first touch. A shared structure holds `MapPtr<T>` and never `T`, which is what makes it `Sync` by construction rather than by review. Compiled code reaches per-request state as `map_base + const` after the tier's crossing has touched every map pointer the trace names, in safe Rust, on first entry per request.

**Identities.** A class or a method published with a unit, or by the inheritance cache, is named by a row of the engine's published arena. `ClassId` and `Function` stay one `u32` whose top bit says whether it names a published row or one of the request's own. So a published record holds its holders, parent and methods directly, and a request reads them with no lock. A request declares a published class by one insert of its handle into its class table, as `zend_accel_class_hash_copy` does. The arena is append-only: no row is freed or reused while the engine runs. A unit the cache replaces leaves its rows behind as wasted memory, counted as `ZSMMG(wasted_shared_memory)` counts it, until an opcache restart (`opcache_reset`, or `opcache.max_wasted_percentage` reached) clears the arena once no request holds its epoch. A map pointer names per-request state of a published row and never names the row.

**Published, and the word.** The unit cache stores `Published`: the immutable `PublishedUnit` beside the engine's `Func` for each of its op arrays and their safepoint words. The map-pointer offset assigned at publication is each op array's own, carried on the op array as `zend_op_array` carries its `ZEND_MAP_PTR(run_time_cache)`, so a frame reaches it with no hop through `Published`. `Func`, and so the words, live in `Published` and not in the unit (ADR-0001). A `Word` is one opaque value per safepoint, two `AtomicU32` halves behind one accessor, all zero at publication; `php_engine` knows this much and no more, and `Nop` never reads one. A request-scoped op array, from `eval`, an uncached include or a closure declared in one, has a `Func` in request memory whose `published()` is `None` and whose words are **sealed**, all ones, the one bit pattern `php_engine` knows: the tier reads a sealed word as never compilable and never claims on it, so no path on the tier's side reaches for a `Published` that does not exist. There is no per-request feedback word; heat, compiled state and blacklist are the engine-level word, and type feedback is the trace record (ADR-0006). The per-request alternative is written down in `jit-design.md` §3.3 with F.3 as its criterion.

A sort of the ini table, which php makes in place on the first `phpinfo()` or `ini_get_all()` (`Z/Zend/zend_ini.c:195`), is the request's (`IniOrder`, `Ini::sort_entries`), and every request starts in registration order. That is php's answer for the CLI and the CGI, and for a fresh php-fpm worker or ZTS thread. php's carry-over into a worker's later requests depends on which worker served the earlier one, history this engine does not keep: `Engine` is immutable after start (§3.4, ADR-0005), and a request's output depends only on what that request sent (#2986).

---

## 6. Compilation and the unit cache


**A request's locale** (revision 40). `setlocale()` changes the process's locale in php, which one request per process makes safe; here requests share a process on threads, so a request's locale is its own state: `setlocale()` builds a POSIX-2008 `locale_t` with `newlocale()` from the categories it names and keeps it on the request, and the request's thread installs it with `uselocale()` while that request runs and restores the thread's own after, so no other request sees it. The natives that read the locale (`strcoll` under `SORT_LOCALE_STRING`, the `*printf` family's `%f`, `localeconv`, `nl_langinfo`, the ctype classes) then run the C library's own locale-aware code under the thread's installed locale, php's algorithm and output, while what php holds locale-independent (casts, `var_export`, `json_encode`, `number_format`) stays C whatever is installed. A fiber or a native that moves the request to another thread carries its `locale_t` with it. The process's `LC_CTYPE` set once at startup (t976, `php_module_startup`'s `zend_reset_lc_ctype_locale`) is the base a request's `locale_t` starts from.
### 6.1 Compiler, and its oracle

One pass over the typed tree, structured after `zend_compile.c`: the same evaluation-order decisions, the same temporaries, the same `ROPE` compilation of interpolated strings, the same `FAST_CALL`/`FAST_RET` layout for `finally`, the same delayed early binding. Constant expression evaluation handles 8.5's closures, first-class callables and casts in constant expressions.

**Op-array identity is a measured claim.** php prints its own op arrays before the optimizer runs:

```
php -n -d opcache.enable_cli=1 -d opcache.file_cache_only=1 -d opcache.file_cache=<dir> \
    -d opcache.opt_debug_level=0x10000 file.php
```

The output (`research/data/r2-php-oparray-dump.txt`) is one block per op array: opline number, result operand, opcode, operands with kinds and literal values, jump targets, and the live-range table. `Unit::dump()` prints the same format, and `xtask compile-identity` diffs the two over a corpus through `oracle::compare`. **Gate: byte-identical on every file of `Zend/tests`, `tests/` and `sapi/cli` per commit (≈ 6.5k files), and on the top-1000 Packagist corpus nightly (172k files).** Any deliberate difference is a row of kind `compile` in the divergence registry; the target is zero. What this buys: evaluation-order and temporary-lifetime bugs of the R127 kind are visible in the live-range table before a single opline executes; the `SMART_BRANCH` question of §5.8 was settled by the dump; and the compiler lane can reach green independently of the VM lane, on day 3, against an oracle.

**No optimizer runs on an executed op array** (revision 44, #1220). What executes is what the compiler emits and `compile-identity` holds: php's op arrays before `zend_optimize_script`. `Zend/Optimizer` is read and not run: its `zend_may_throw_ex` is the source of `ops.toml`'s `throw` effect, and the compiled tier may take its per-opline transfer functions (`zend_update_type_info`) for a trace's types, which rewrite nothing (`jit-design.md` §4.2). `opcache.optimization_level` and `opcache.opt_debug_level` keep php's names and defaults; `0x10000` prints the compiler's op arrays and no other dump bit prints anything. If an optimizer is ever built it is a port of `Zend/Optimizer`, run at publication between the compiler and `Published`, in a crate of its own, held to php's `0x20000` dump by a gate of its own and to the safepoint assertion on its output; never passes of the engine's own.

**The lane dumps through the engine's binary.** `xtask` links no workspace crate: it reaches the engine through the binaries it builds and starts, `php-linked` and the hosts' own (harness revision 84). `compile-identity` runs the engine's binary under the oracle's dump recipe, `-l` and php's dump flags, which the binary honours as php does, printing `Unit::dump`'s bytes for each file of the corpus; nothing stands between the compiler and the diff but the bytes it printed. Where no engine binary is built the lane dumps nothing and its reading says why. Revision 10's in-process way, through `php_bundle`, is gone with the harness's edge to it (#2166, #2213).

**Compile-time errors live in the compiler.** Each of Mahout's validate checks is a guard at the compiler function that owns the construct (`compile_class_decl`, `compile_func_decl`, `compile_params`, `compile_break_continue`, and so on), fed by the compiler's scope instead of ancestor climbs. Each guard emits what `zend_error_noreturn` takes: an error kind, php's exact text, a byte offset. php reports the first fatal it *reaches* in compile order, which is not always source order; a compiler that checks at the site reproduces that by construction. The seven lexical fatals (`(real)` and `(unset)` casts, `$s{0}`, `(void)` placement, nested `const`, octal overflow, and peers) belong to the parser and are emitted there with `E_PARSE`. Mahout's 10,228-line validation fixture file is re-homed as compile-output fixtures. There is no code number on a diagnostic; the engine has one voice. A compile-time diagnostic that is not a fatal — the NAN coercion warning a fold raises, and the compile-time deprecations — is recorded on the unit as `Unit::diagnostics`, php's `zend_error_info` less the file, and raised by the engine through the path a runtime diagnostic takes, in the unit's own file at the compiler's line, before the main op array runs, on every request the unit serves. That is opcache's `persistent_script.warnings` replayed by `zend_emit_recorded_errors_ex` on a cache hit, and it is what the oracle does with no cache at all. `Level` is `php_unit`'s, so the compiler can name it; `api::raising` re-exports it where extensions already read it.

**Safepoints.** The compiler emits `OpArray.safepoints` and writes each site's number into the field `ops.toml` names (§4.7, §5.7). Identity against php's dump is unchanged, because the dump prints no unused field. **The compiler asserts, on every op array it emits, that every backward jump's opcode has a `safepoint` column and that its target is a numbered header**; the assertion fails `php_compile`'s own tests and the `compile-identity` corpus sweep, so a loop that would silently never compile cannot exist. The gate that the numbers are right is `php_engine`'s: every backward jump and return in a witness carries the number of a word, two back-edges to one header carry one number, and the entry is zero.

Compile speed target: within 2× of php's for the top-1000 corpus, measured; it matters only on cache miss.

### 6.2 Class linking

Per-unit class declarations are extracted at compile time. A class whose parent, interfaces and traits are resolvable within the unit or already linked is bound early into an **immutable linked class** (`Sync`, shared across requests). Otherwise `DECLARE_CLASS_DELAYED` links at run time, as opcache's inheritance cache links. The link first looks for a published linked class keyed on the unlinked declaration and on the published identities of its parent, interfaces, traits and variance dependencies (`zend_accel_inheritance_cache_find`). Where every one of those is published, it publishes what it links. A class with any request-owned dependency links into a request-owned class. The linker is `zend_inheritance.c` over `php_unit` declarations for user and native classes alike: property and constant inheritance, method compatibility (variance with the 8.5 relaxation of `static` to `self` in final classes and `Closure` as a subtype of `callable`), trait binding **before** parent (8.5 order change), abstract/interface checks, enum backing, hook inheritance, `#[\Override]`, `#[\Deprecated]` and `#[\ReturnTypeWillChange]` validation, delayed variance obligations.

**The linker is `php_link::link`, one function for both callers.** The compiler runs it from its early bind, as `zend_try_early_bind` does. Its outcome decides the op array: an early-bound class, or a `DECLARE_CLASS` or `DECLARE_CLASS_DELAYED` when a class it needs is not yet known. A refusal there is a compile-time `E_COMPILE_ERROR`. The engine runs the same function when it links at run time. No second copy of a check exists in `php_compile` or `php_engine` (revision 33).

**Method compatibility has php's third outcome.** The check returns error, success or *warning*; warning is what an incompatible or missing child return against a tentative parent return produces (`Z/Zend/zend_inheritance.c:872-891`). A warning is rendered as `E_DEPRECATED` at the child's declaration site with php's text, "Return type of %s should either be compatible with %s, or the #[\ReturnTypeWillChange] attribute should be used to temporarily suppress the notice", unless the child carries `#[\ReturnTypeWillChange]`, in which case nothing is raised. A parameter mismatch against a tentative parent is still an error, because the bit is on the return. Delayed variance keeps the three-way outcome, so `Zend/tests/tentative_type_early_binding.phpt` reproduces without a special case. `#[\ReturnTypeWillChange]` targets methods only and has no other effect.

**The linked tables are the only metadata readers see.** Reflection, `var_dump`/`print_r`/`var_export`, `serialize`, attribute instantiation and the cross-check's rendering read the linked tables and nothing else: no declaration model after link, no fields on the object header, no re-evaluation of defaults from source text. Reflection reads the tentative bit from the linked table: `hasReturnType()` and `getReturnType()` answer for a real return type only; `hasTentativeReturnType()` and `getTentativeReturnType()` for a tentative one; `__toString()` prints "Tentative return" in the return row. Closure and generator introspection is a small accessor set on the engine, read through `api`. Defaults are constant-expression trees evaluated by the engine on request and reported unevaluated to Reflection.

### 6.3 Unit cache and `opcache_*`

The unit cache is an `api::Cache` under another name, keyed by path with its stamp on the held file, as *Keying and validation* below says (revision 45), and the two share their implementation (§4.4). Its value is `Published` (§5.14): op arrays, each carrying its run-time cache size and the map-pointer offset assigned at publication, literals (interned), functions with their words, classes (early-bound where possible) and source map. Cache hit means zero compile work per request, which is where Mahout lost 64 ms per request against php's 2.4 ms. `opcache_get_status`, `opcache_get_configuration`, `opcache_reset`, `opcache_invalidate`, `opcache_compile_file`, `opcache_is_script_cached`, `opcache.preload` answer from this cache, since 8.5 makes opcache always present. Under uid 0 with `opcache.preload_user` naming another user, php preloads in a forked child that has taken that user's gid, supplementary groups and uid (`accel_finish_startup_preload_subprocess`); the engine serves on threads of one process (§3.4), so the preload runs on one thread of its own that takes those credentials for itself alone before any PHP runs, and every other thread keeps the process's. On a platform with no per-thread credentials the engine refuses to start rather than preload as root (#1258). An evicted unit is wasted memory, as opcache's is: the epoch keeps it, with the rows it published, until the opcache restart that clears the epoch once no request holds it (§5.14 *Identities*). So nothing a request, a trace or a compile job of that epoch can reach is freed before the epoch is (`jit-design.md` §4.5). An on-disk serialised form (`opcache.file_cache` equivalent) is month two. An include's memory is held to opcache's regime, the model this cache copies: an include of a unit the cache holds costs the request what opcache's `persistent_compile_file` lookup allocates there, and a witness of an include's `memory_get_usage()` or `memory_get_peak_usage()` names `[regime.jit-off]`. Under the recipe's `php -n` php compiles every include into the request's arena, a difference §5.13's approximation states and the registry's `witness:cf4528199e…` row accepts (#1483).

**Keying and validation** (revision 45, #1228). The unit cache is keyed as php's `ZCSG(hash)` is: by the file's full path, and by the path as written with the include path and the working directory beside it where it is relative, while `opcache.revalidate_path` is off (`accel_make_persistent_key`, `persistent_zend_resolve_path`); the stamp php validates, `st_mtime` in whole seconds, is on the held file and not in the key. Whether a hit is validated is the answer to the one question the cache asks an extension about a file, which the blacklist becomes (`ExtensionBuilder::admission`): kept out; cached and never validated (`opcache.validate_timestamps=0`); cached and validated once per `opcache.revalidate_freq` seconds, the next due time an atomic on the held file (`validate_timestamp_and_record`); or cached and validated on every hit, which is what a request whose accelerator is off, or a bundle with no extension that answers, gets. A held unit is never changed: a failed validation compiles and replaces it, and whoever holds the old one keeps it (principle 4).

---

## 7. Extensions

### 7.1 The ABI

A native is a Rust item with an attribute on it and nothing else. `#[php_function]`, `#[php_class]` and `#[php_const]`, on `fn`, `struct`, `enum`, `impl`, `const` and inline `mod`, with the inert `#[php(..)]` they read, derive a native's declaration from its Rust types and identifiers and expand to `::php_ext::abi::` and `::php_ext::__private::` paths (`crate::api::` in `php_engine`, for Core; ADR-0007). No string anywhere carries a signature, a type or a name. The rules:

- **Types are traits, not tokens.** A parameter's declared type, by-reference and variadic bits, its hold and its absent path are items of `FromArgument`; a return's declared type and conversion are `IntoReturn`'s. The macro emits `<T as FromArgument>::declared()` and matches no type by name, so a mistaken type is a Rust type error and a PHP type php itself refuses (`?mixed`, `bool|false`, a duplicate member) is a `const` assertion over the type's mask.
- **Names are identifiers.** The PHP name is the Rust identifier with `r#` stripped, byte for byte (`r#type`, `qualifiedName`, `PDO`, `php_user_filter`), and the macro allows the casing lints on what it re-emits. No key renames.
- **The entry is php's ZPP.** `zend_wrong_parameters_count_error`'s arity first, then each argument left to right through `coerce::argument`, the engine's `zend_parse_arg_*` with the caller's `strict_types` and the 8.1 null deprecation, then the body, then the return; there is no Plain/Reentrant channel, because re-entry is a parameter (principle 9), and the entry takes `php_engine`'s concrete `Request` (ADR-0002). Holding an argument takes no owner and makes no copy, so collection timing is php's; the user code coercion runs (`__toString`, autoload, the error handler) is php's own, in php's order. A native whose php counterpart does work of its own before `zend_parse_parameters`, as ext/intl's `INTL_METHOD_INIT_VARS` resets the request's error, names it with `before_args = path`, a `fn(&mut Request)` the entry calls before the arity.
- **Optionality and nullability are two facts.** `Option<T>` is `?T`; `#[php(default = …)]` makes a parameter optional; `Passed<T>` is `= UNKNOWN`, or presence the body observes beside a shown default. A default is php's constant expression written as tokens and read as php's lexer reads them (`0777` octal, `0o644` explicit octal, `" \n\r\t\x0B\0"`, `A | B`, `self::X`, `Class::class`, `Ns::Class::CONST` for `\Ns\Class::CONST`), stored unevaluated and evaluated by the engine when the argument is absent (§4.7).
- **Escape hatches are types.** `Raw<T>` declares `T` and lets the body run the declared coercion when it chooses; `Untyped<T>` declares nothing and holds as `T` (the `@param resource` and by-reference out parameters); `#[php_function(arity = manual)]` leaves the count to the body. Each still derives the declaration, so none can say what the body does not.
- **Classes.** A `#[php_class(..)]` struct or enum is the header (kind, flags, parent, interfaces, payload, handlers, namespace) and one `#[php_class]` impl is the members, registered by `ExtensionBuilder::class::<C>()`, whose `Members` bound makes a forgotten impl a build error. A method without a `This<Self>` receiver is static. A body-less `fn` is an interface method, an abstract method, or, with `implementation = Other::method`, php's `@implementation-alias`, and a body-less method in a class whose header is neither interface nor abstract is a `const` assertion's build error. A Rust `enum` is a PHP enum whose variants are its cases and which bodies take and return as the Rust variant. A property is an associated `const` marked `#[php(property ..)]`, its value checked against the property's type; a class constant is a plain associated `const`, emitted and typed by value; `#[php_class(extern)]` names a class another extension or Core declares, and an impl on it routes its constants to `class_constants` and, under `trampolines`, its methods to `trampolines`. PHP visibility is `#[php(protected)]` and `#[php(private)]`, public by default; Rust visibility keeps its own meaning.
- **Order is written.** Functions and classes register in the order the `extension()` value lists them, one line each, a `#[cfg]` on the line where php's stub has an `#if`; members keep source order within their kind.

`php_macros` is the one crate that could be replaced without touching an extension body, and `php_ext` is the one crate every body names.

An `Extension` is a value: its declarations paired with entries, its constants, its ini entries with defaults and on-modify hooks, its lifecycle functions, and its three state declarations, one per scope, `process_state(fn() -> Result<P, Refusal>)`, `engine_state(fn(&Start) -> Result<E, Refusal>)` and `request_state::<T: Default>()`, none required. Each `ext/<name>` crate exports one `extension()`; `php_bundle::enabled()` collects them behind its features in module-startup order; `Engine::builder().extensions(..)` takes the slice. Nothing in the bundle links, orders classes, or runs a hook. Lifecycle order is the startup order, the bundle's as `zend_sort_modules` leaves it, and its reverse, for construction, `rinit`, `rshutdown` and drop alike. An extension's lifecycle is three scopes, process, engine and request, that start as values and end as phases (ADR-0010). The starts are the three state declarations. Each extension's process start runs, the first time, immediately before its engine start, in bundle order; the engine start reaches its own process state and those of the extensions before it. A refused process start is kept for the process: every later engine carrying the extension is refused with it, and the start is not called again. The ends are phases in reverse startup order, each extension's hook in a `zend_try` of its own: the request ends with `rshutdown`, then `deactivate`, then `post_deactivate`, after the request's objects and resources are freed and with no PHP code allowed to run, every request state still live through all three, and then the request states drop; the engine ends with the `engine_end` hooks, which the drop of the engine's last clone serves once on a shutdown request the engine lays for it, and then the engine states drop; the process scope has no end, since process exit releases it. A request whose start failed is served no request-end phase, except under the built-in server, which serves them after a failed start as php's `php_cli_server_dispatch` does (`Z/sapi/cli/php_cli_server.c:2307-2310`); every other host does not. A state's `Drop` runs after every `post_deactivate` hook and may touch no engine.

The type vocabulary is wide enough that overrides and raw bodies go to zero: the scalars and `PhpArray`, `PhpObject`, `Value` for `mixed`, `Callable`, `Iterable`, `Resource`; `Option<T>`, `OrFalse<T>`, `Union<A, B>` nested to n-ary with class members written in php's order, since `declared_type_name` keeps it; `Instance<C>` for a class, `Static`, `Never`, `True`, `False`, `Null`; `Ref<T>` for every by-reference shape, its referent held on entry and `Ref<PhpArray>::separated` changing the caller's array in place; `Variadic<T>` and `NamedVariadic<T>`; `This<C>` for the receiver; and `#[php(tentative)]` on a method's return. Mahout's 218 signature overrides at the census pin (443 on its later tree, 62% of them spellable in its own vocabulary and written only because bodies returned `Value`), 88 bare-`Value` parameters and 142 hand-marshaled natives are the measure of what a narrow vocabulary costs.

**The cross-check is new work, and it is a rendering diff.** `xtask crosscheck` serves `echo new ReflectionExtension('<name>');` through `php-linked`, the bundle with every feature on in a process the harness starts (`xtask` links no workspace crate), which links each extension's declarations into the linked tables and renders the extension as `php --re <name>` does, `ReflectionExtension::__toString()` over the linked tables (§6.2), and holds the bytes to the oracle's `php --re <name>` under the recipe: every constant, ini entry, function, class, method, property and the tentative-return row, in php's own wording and order, byte for byte. The oracle's bytes are the verdict and the engine's rendering is the claim, so the linked tables, the linker and `ext/reflection` are all under the one reading. A native whose return type is real where php's is tentative renders "Return" where the oracle renders "Tentative return", and would make the engine fatal where php deprecates; the reverse would make it deprecate where php fatals. Either is a red build. `--ext <name>` runs one. The stub diff, each crate's declaration builders against `php-src/ext/<name>/*.stub.php`, is not built: it needs a stub parser this repository does not keep and measures the builders alone, the weaker form of the same sensor, and the stubs are read by nothing. The same sensor's later halves are the `--ri <name>` column, php's MINFO table through `php_info_print_module`, and the binary's own `--rf`, `--rc`, `--re` and `--ri` flags (`sapi/cli/php_cli.c:1036-1091` at the tag), which print the same renderings.

There is no `dl()` and no C `zend_module_entry` ABI; both predecessors ruled this out and the ruling stands because it would require Zend's internal ABI, not just its behaviour. `ext/ffi` calls C through libffi and loads no extension, so this ruling does not reach it (§7.2, revision 75). An `extension=` or `zend_extension=` line is loaded where php loads it, after startup, through php's path building for its kind, and fails as a load with no shared object fails, with php's warning naming the paths it tried, a bundled extension's name included (revision 57, `trace:ini-extension-no-loader`).

Four more refusals stand beside it (revision 83, #1780):

- No extension replaces an opcode's handler (`zend_set_user_opcode_handler` returning anything but `ZEND_USER_OPCODE_DISPATCH`): every semantic lives in one function called from one loop (§3, principle 2: one executor). An extension may observe a declared set of opcodes, and the handler always runs. The set is fixed as the engine starts, so the engine chooses its loop there, as php resolves an opline's handler when it compiles it: one loop that tells the set's observers before each of their oplines, and, where no extension declared an opcode, one with no test for it (§5.8, revision 107).
- No extension rewrites a syntax tree in place (`zend_ast_process`): a unit is a function of its source and its compile identity, compiled once and shared by every engine in the process (§3, principle 4: immutable after publication), and a tree rewritten per extension would put the extension set in every unit's identity. An extension that wants different code compiles different source.
- No extension replaces the allocator (`zend_mm_set_custom_handlers`): `memory_get_usage()` and `gc_status()` are php's algorithm (§3, principle 1: Zend's algorithms), and a replacement changes them. A profiler observes allocations instead.
- There is no Zend-extension message handler: no Zend extension is loaded at run time, so no new-extension message is ever sent; the bundle is whole at engine build.

**php's `zend_extension` row, handler by handler** (#2038). A Zend extension declares its row on its `Extension` value as `abi::ZendExtension`, and of the handlers php's row carries (`Z/Zend/zend_extensions.h:91-104`) one is built and the rest are refused. `statement_handler` is built (#1823): at each statement of a unit compiled with extended info, which `-e` turns on as php's does, `ZEND_EXT_STMT` tells every Zend extension's handler in startup order, with `EG(no_extensions)` set while a host's own string is served (`Z/Zend/zend_execute_API.c:1357`, `:1382`); no php-src extension sets it, and its users are step debuggers. `fcall_begin_handler` and `fcall_end_handler` are refused, and the observer (seam 9) is their equivalent: `ZEND_EXT_FCALL_BEGIN` and `ZEND_EXT_FCALL_END` (`Z/Zend/zend_vm_def.h:7935-7957`) go on to the next opline and tell nothing. The observer differs from them, and a tool ported from them meets the differences: their hook runs in the caller's frame, after the arguments are sent and before `DO_*CALL`, with the callee's frame laid down as `EX(call)` but not current (`Z/Zend/zend_compile.c:3977`, `:4000`); it sees compiled call sites, `include` and `eval` only (`:10806`), never a callback from a native, a magic method or a destructor; it runs only under `-e`; and the end handler is handed no return value. `op_array_handler`, `op_array_ctor`, `op_array_dtor`, `op_array_persist_calc` and `op_array_persist` are refused: each hands an extension php's `zend_op_array` to read, extend or rewrite as it is compiled, cached or freed, which a unit compiled once and shared by every engine in the process cannot allow (§3, principle 4: immutable after publication), and which presupposes the Zend C ABI §7.1 already rules out.

**Zend's own natives, classes and constants are an `Extension` value named `Core`, numbered zero.** php registers `zend_builtin_module`, the `zend_module_entry` named `Core`, in `zend_startup_builtin_functions` from `zend_startup`, before any extension's `MINIT`, and asserts it takes number zero (`Z/Zend/zend_builtin_functions.c:46-68`, `Z/Zend/zend.c:1058`); `get_loaded_extensions()` and `php -m` list it first. The engine makes the same value at startup, before the bundle's first: the classes, functions and constants `php_engine` declares itself, under the name php gives them, with no crate under `ext/`, no `[[crate]]` row in the manifest and no feature on `php_bundle`, so the empty bundle still carries `Core` and a Zend-only script sees what php's does. What follows from one value rather than a name: `ReflectionClass::getExtension()` and `ReflectionFunctionAbstract::getExtension()` on a Zend class or native answer the `Core` reflector, `new ReflectionExtension('Core')` succeeds, `ReflectionFunction::__toString()` prints `<internal:Core>`, and `php --re Core` is one more rendering `xtask crosscheck` holds. `Core` is not a name the api answers for what no extension declared: every native and class has an extension, and a name with no value behind it is what this paragraph rules out (revision 25, #379).

**The tier is not an extension.** It declares its state and ini through `Tier::extension()` so that seam 3 and §4.4 carry them, but `php_bundle` lists it as the tier alias, not in `enabled()`, and `get_loaded_extensions()` does not report it, as php does not report its JIT.

### 7.2 The library rule, applied
